[Presidential elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_presidential_election) were held in the [United States](3434750.md) on November 3, 2020.[^cite_note-ElectionDay-1] The [Democratic](5043544.md) ticket of former [vice president](32759.md) [Joe Biden](145422.md) and [California](5407.md) junior [senator](24909346.md) [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris) defeated the incumbent [Republican](32070.md) [president](24113.md) [Donald Trump](4848272.md) and vice president [Mike Pence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Pence).[^cite_note-12] The election saw the highest [voter turnout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter_turnout_in_United_States_presidential_elections) by percentage [since 1900](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1900_United_States_presidential_election). Biden received more than 81 million votes,[^cite_note-\:7-13] the [most votes ever cast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_presidential_candidates_by_number_of_votes_received) for a presidential candidate in U.S. history.[^cite_note-\:8-14]

In a competitive primary that featured the most candidates for any political party in the [modern era of American politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sixth_Party_System), Biden secured the [Democratic presidential nomination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Democratic_Party_presidential_primaries). Biden's running mate, Harris, became the first African American, first Asian American, and third female[^cite_note-15] vice presidential nominee on a major party ticket. Trump easily secured re-nomination with only minor opposition in the [Republican primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Republican_Party_presidential_primaries). [Jo Jorgensen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jo_Jorgensen) secured the [Libertarian presidential nomination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Libertarian_Party_presidential_primaries) with [Spike Cohen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spike_Cohen) as her running mate, and [Howie Hawkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howie_Hawkins) secured the [Green presidential nomination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Green_Party_presidential_primaries) with [Angela Nicole Walker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angela_Nicole_Walker) as his running mate.

The central issues of the election included the public health and [economic impacts of the COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_the_United_States), [civil unrest in reaction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Floyd_protests) to the police [murder of George Floyd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murder_of_George_Floyd), the [Supreme Court](31737.md) following the [death of Ruth Bader Ginsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_Ruth_Bader_Ginsburg) and [confirmation of Amy Coney Barrett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amy_Coney_Barrett_Supreme_Court_nomination), and the future of the [Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affordable_Care_Act).[^cite_note-16] Due to the ongoing pandemic, a record number of ballots were [cast early](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_voting) and [by mail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postal_voting).[^cite_note-\:9-17] Thirty-eight states had over half of all votes cast using these methods, and only three states had fewer than 25%.[^cite_note-18] As a result of a large number of mail-in ballots, some [swing states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swing_state) saw delays in vote counting and reporting; this led to major news outlets delaying their projection of Biden and Harris as the [president-elect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_transition_of_Joe_Biden) and [vice president-elect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vice_president-elect_of_the_United_States) until the morning of November 7, 2020.[^cite_note-math-19]

Biden defeated Trump, winning 306 [Electoral College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Electoral_College) votes and 51.3% of the popular vote to Trump's 232 electoral votes and 46.8% of the popular vote. Trump was the first president to lose re-election since [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush) in [1992](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_United_States_presidential_election). Key to Biden's pivotal victory were his wins in the Democratic-leaning [Rust Belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rust_Belt) states of [Michigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election_in_Michigan), [Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election_in_Pennsylvania), and [Wisconsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election_in_Wisconsin), which Trump narrowly carried in 2016 and whose combined 46 electoral votes were enough to swing the election to either candidate. This election marked the first time since [1948](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_United_States_presidential_election) that the Democratic candidate won the popular vote in four consecutive elections.

Trump refused to accept the results; he and his allies made disproven claims of fraud, pressured election officials, filed several unsuccessful lawsuits,[^cite_note-20][^cite_note-21][^cite_note-22] and directly [attempted to overturn the results](65905202.md) at the county, state, and federal level. This culminated in an [attack on the United States Capitol](66307490.md) on January 6, 2021, for which [Trump was impeached a second time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_impeachment_of_Donald_Trump). The day after the attack, Trump stated that a "new administration" would be succeeding his, without mentioning president-elect Biden by name, in a video posted on [Twitter](9988187.md).[^cite_note-23][^cite_note-24][^cite_note-25]

## Background

Further information: [United States presidential election § Procedure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_presidential_election#Procedure)



[Donald Trump](4848272.md), the incumbent president in 2020, whose first non-consecutive term expired at noon on January 20, 2021

The [Maine Legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maine_Legislature) passed a bill in August 2019 adopting [ranked-choice voting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ranked-choice_voting_in_the_United_States) (RCV) both for presidential primaries and for the general election.[^cite_note-ovz9x-26][^cite_note-qhJpi-27] Governor [Janet Mills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janet_Mills) allowed the bill to become law without her signature, which delayed its taking effect until after the [2020 Democratic primary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Maine_Democratic_primary) in March and made Maine the first state to use RCV for a presidential general election. The [Maine Republican Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maine_Republican_Party) filed signatures for a [veto referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veto_referendum) to preclude the use of RCV for the 2020 election, but [Secretary of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maine_Secretary_of_State) [Matthew Dunlap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matthew_Dunlap) found there were insufficient valid signatures to qualify for the ballot. A challenge in [Maine Superior Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maine_Superior_Court) was successful for the Maine Republican Party, but the [Maine Supreme Judicial Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maine_Supreme_Judicial_Court)[^cite_note-28][^cite_note-29] stayed the ruling pending appeal on September 8, 2020.[^cite_note-30] Nevertheless, ballots began being printed later that day without the veto referendum and including RCV for the presidential election,[^cite_note-31][^cite_note-forbes-32] and the Court ruled in favor of the secretary of state on September 22, allowing RCV to be used.[^cite_note-33] An emergency appeal to the [Supreme Court](31737.md) was denied on October 6.[^cite_note-34] The law continues the use of the [congressional district method](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congressional_district_method) for the allocation of Maine's electors ([Nebraska](21647.md) is the only other state that apportions its electoral votes this way).[^cite_note-FcJYG-35] While multiple rounds of vote counting were not needed due to a single candidate receiving a majority of first-choice votes statewide and in each district, use of RCV complicates interpretation of the national [popular vote](652563.md) because voters are more likely to vote for third-party or independent candidates.[^cite_note-36]

On December 14, 2020, pledged electors for each candidate, known collectively as the [United States Electoral College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Electoral_College), gathered in their states' capitols to cast their official votes. Pursuant to the processes laid out by the [Electoral Count Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_Count_Act) of 1887, [certificates of ascertainment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Certificate_of_ascertainment) listing the names of the electors and separate certificates recording their votes are distributed to various officials across the branches of government.[^cite_note-37][^cite_note-38][^cite_note-39] The newly elected Congress, with the vice president in his role as Senate president presiding, met in a [joint session](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_session_of_the_United_States_Congress) to formally open the certificates and count the votes, which began on January 6, 2021, was interrupted by the [January 6 United States Capitol attack](66307490.md), and finished the following day.[^cite_note-40]

### Democratic Party

Main articles: [2020 Democratic Party presidential primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Democratic_Party_presidential_primaries), [2020 Democratic Party vice presidential candidate selection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Democratic_Party_vice_presidential_candidate_selection), [2020 Democratic National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Democratic_National_Convention), and [Joe Biden 2020 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Biden_2020_presidential_campaign)

The Democratic Party chose its nominee in the [2020 Democratic Party presidential primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Democratic_Party_presidential_primaries). [Joe Biden](145422.md) became the [presumptive nominee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presumptive_nominee_\(United_States\)) of the Democratic Party on April 8, 2020, after senator [Bernie Sanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernie_Sanders) of [Vermont](32578.md) withdrew from the race.[^cite_note-41] On June 5, 2020, Biden secured enough delegates to ensure his nomination at the national convention.[^cite_note-42] Biden picked [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris) as his vice-presidential nominee, and the ticket was formally nominated at the convention on August 18.[^cite_note-43]

Incumbent president [Donald Trump](4848272.md) and incumbent vice president Mike Pence were able to easily secure the nomination against former governor of [Massachusetts](1645518.md) [Bill Weld](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Weld) after Trump received enough delegates in the [2020 Republican presidential primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Republican_Party_presidential_primaries). They were formally nominated at the Republican National Convention on August 24, 2020.[^cite_note-44][^cite_note-45] Trump won 2,549 delegates (out of 2,550), one of the most in [presidential primary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_presidential_primary) history.[^cite_note-46]

### Libertarian Party

Main articles: [2020 Libertarian Party presidential primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Libertarian_Party_presidential_primaries), [2020 Libertarian National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Libertarian_National_Convention), and [Jo Jorgensen 2020 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jo_Jorgensen_2020_presidential_campaign)

[Jo Jorgensen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jo_Jorgensen), who was the running mate of author [Harry Browne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_Browne) in [1996](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_United_States_presidential_election), received the Libertarian nomination at the national convention on May 23, 2020.[^cite_note-Jorgensen_nomination-47] She achieved ballot access in all 50 states and the District of Columbia.[^cite_note-Jorgensen_ballot_access-48]

### Green Party

Main articles: [2020 Green Party presidential primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Green_Party_presidential_primaries), [2020 Green National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Green_National_Convention), and [Howie Hawkins 2020 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howie_Hawkins_2020_presidential_campaign)

[Howie Hawkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howie_Hawkins) became the presumptive nominee of the Green Party on June 21, 2020, and was officially nominated by the party on July 11, 2020.[^cite_note-49][^cite_note-50] Hawkins secured ballot access in 29 states and the District of Columbia, representing 381 electoral votes, and write-in access in 16 more states, representing 130 electoral votes.[^cite_note-51][^cite_note-52][^cite_note-54]

### Party conventions

The [2020 Democratic National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Democratic_National_Convention) was originally scheduled for July 13–16 in [Milwaukee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee), [Wisconsin](33127.md),[^cite_note-58][^cite_note-59][^cite_note-OJaOg-60] but was delayed to August 17–20 due to the effects of the [COVID-19 pandemic](63136490.md).[^cite_note-61] On June 24, 2020, it was announced that the convention would be held in a mixed online in-person format, with most delegates attending remotely but a few still attending the physical convention site.[^cite_note-62] On August 5, the in-person portion of the convention was scaled down even further; major speeches, including Biden's, were switched to a virtual format.[^cite_note-63]

The [2020 Republican National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Republican_National_Convention) took place from August 24–27 in [Charlotte, North Carolina](57447.md), and various remote locations. Originally, a three-day convention was planned to be held in North Carolina, but due to North Carolina's insistence that the convention follow COVID-19 social distancing rules, the speeches and celebrations were moved to [Jacksonville, Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacksonville,_Florida) (official convention business was still contractually obligated to be conducted in Charlotte).[^cite_note-64][^cite_note-65] Due to the worsening situation with regards to COVID-19 in Florida, the plans there were cancelled, and the convention was moved back to Charlotte in a scaled-down capacity.[^cite_note-66]

The [2020 Libertarian National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Libertarian_National_Convention) was originally scheduled to be held in [Austin, Texas](1998.md), over [Memorial Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memorial_Day) weekend from May 22 to 25,[^cite_note-67][^cite_note-68] but all reservations at the [JW Marriott Downtown Austin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JW_Marriott_Hotels) for the convention were cancelled on April 26 due to the COVID-19 pandemic.[^cite_note-69] The Libertarian National Committee eventually decided the party would hold two conventions, one online from May 22–24 to select the presidential and vice-presidential nominees and one at a physical convention in [Orlando, Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orlando,_Florida), from July 8–12 for other business.[^cite_note-70]

The [2020 Green National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Green_National_Convention) was originally to be held in [Detroit](8687.md), Michigan, from July 9 to 12.[^cite_note-OJaOg-60] Due to the COVID-19 pandemic, the convention was instead held online, without a change in date.[^cite_note-71]

### Issues unique to the election

#### Impeachment

Further information: [First impeachment of Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_impeachment_of_Donald_Trump)

The House of Representatives [voted to impeach Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_impeachment_of_Donald_Trump) on two counts on December 18, 2019.[^cite_note-OB3KB-72] The trial in the Senate began on January 21, 2020,[^cite_note-IIWDj-73] and ended on February 5, resulting in [acquittal](300834.md#United_States) by the [United States Senate](24909346.md).[^cite_note-74]

This is the [second](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1868_Democratic_National_Convention) time a president has been impeached during his first term while running for a second term.[^cite_note-75][^cite_note-77] Trump continued to hold campaign rallies during the impeachment.[^cite_note-FVJtv-78][^cite_note-GGhFA-79] This is also the first time since the modern presidential primaries were established in 1911 that a president has been subjected to impeachment while the primary season was underway.[^cite_note-Politiconov1-80] The impeachment process overlapped with the primary campaigns, forcing senators running for the Democratic nomination to remain in Washington for the trial in the days before and after the Iowa caucuses.[^cite_note-2cIKK-81][^cite_note-JDGyw-82]

#### Effects of the COVID-19 pandemic



States and territories with at least one local, state, or federal primary election date or method of voting altered as of August 5, 2020.

Further information: [COVID-19 pandemic in the United States](63136490.md), [Impact of the COVID-19 pandemic on politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_on_politics), and [White House COVID-19 outbreak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_House_COVID-19_outbreak)

Several events related to the 2020 presidential election were altered or postponed due to the ongoing [COVID-19 pandemic in the United States](63136490.md) and its effects, such as stay-at-home orders and [social distancing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_distancing) guidelines by local governments. On March 10, following primary elections in six states, Democratic candidates Joe Biden and Bernie Sanders cancelled planned campaign night events and further in-person campaigning and campaign rallies.[^cite_note-83][^cite_note-84] On March 12, Trump also stated his intent to postpone further campaign rallies.[^cite_note-85] The 11th Democratic debate was held on March 15 without an audience at the [CNN](62028.md) studios in [Washington, D.C.](108956.md)[^cite_note-86] Several states also postponed their primaries to a later date, including Georgia,[^cite_note-87] Kentucky,[^cite_note-88] Louisiana,[^cite_note-89] Ohio,[^cite_note-90] and Maryland.[^cite_note-91] As of March 24, 2020, all major-party presidential candidates had halted in-person campaigning and campaign rallies over COVID-19 concerns. Political analysts speculated at the time that the moratorium on traditional campaigning, coupled with the effects of the pandemic on the nation, could have unpredictable effects on the voting populace and possibly, how the election will be conducted.[^cite_note-92][^cite_note-93][^cite_note-NYTCampaign-94]

Some presidential primary elections were severely disrupted by COVID-19-related issues, including long lines at polling places, greatly increased requests for absentee ballots, and technology issues.[^cite_note-95] Due to a shortage of election workers able or willing to work during the pandemic, the number of polling places was often greatly reduced. Most states expanded or encouraged voting by mail as an alternative, but many voters complained that they never received the absentee ballots they had requested.[^cite_note-96]

The March 2020 [Coronavirus Aid, Relief, and Economic Security Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coronavirus_Aid,_Relief,_and_Economic_Security_Act) included money for states to increase mail-in voting. By May, Trump and his campaign strongly opposed mail-in voting, claiming that it would cause widespread voter fraud, a belief that has been discredited by a number of media organizations.[^cite_note-auto-97][^cite_note-auto2-98] Government response to the impact of the pandemic from the Trump administration, coupled with the differing positions taken by congressional Democrats and Republicans regarding [economic stimulus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stimulus_\(economics\)), became a major campaign issue for both parties.[^cite_note-NYTCampaign-94][^cite_note-99]

On April 6, the [Supreme Court](31737.md) and Republicans in the State Legislature of Wisconsin rebuffed Wisconsin governor [Tony Evers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Evers)'s request to move [the state's spring elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Wisconsin_elections) to June. As a result, the elections, which included a presidential primary, went ahead on April 7 as planned.[^cite_note-100] At least seven new cases of COVID-19 were traced to this election. Voting-rights advocates expressed fear of similar chaos on a nationwide scale in November, recommending states move to expand vote-by-mail options.[^cite_note-101]

On June 20, 2020, Trump's campaign held [an in-person rally in Tulsa, Oklahoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Trump_Tulsa_rally), after the Oklahoma Supreme Court ruled that the event could go ahead despite continuing concerns over COVID-19.[^cite_note-102] Attendance at the rally was far lower than expected, being described as a "flop", and it led to a significant worsening of relations between Trump and his campaign manager, [Brad Parscale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brad_Parscale).[^cite_note-103] 7.7 million people watched the event on Fox News, a Saturday audience record for that channel.[^cite_note-104] Three weeks after the rally, the Oklahoma State Department of Health recorded record numbers of cases of COVID-19,[^cite_note-105] and former Republican presidential candidate [Herman Cain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herman_Cain) died of the virus, although it was not confirmed that he caught the disease due to his attendance at the rally.[^cite_note-106]

On October 2, 2020, Trump and First Lady [Melania Trump](1209075.md) tested positive for [SARS-CoV-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2) following a positive test from his senior advisor, [Hope Hicks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hope_Hicks), as part of the larger [COVID-19 outbreak among White House personnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_House_COVID-19_outbreak). Both the president and first lady immediately entered quarantine, which prevented Trump from further campaigning, notably at campaign rallies.[^cite_note-COVID_infection_NYT-107][^cite_note-108][^cite_note-109] Later that day, the President was admitted to [Walter Reed National Military Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Reed_National_Military_Medical_Center) with a low grade fever, where he was reported to have received an experimental antibody treatment.[^cite_note-110][^cite_note-111] Trump's diagnosis came only two days after he had shared the stage with Biden at the first presidential debate and raised the possibility that Biden had caught the virus from Trump; Biden tested negative.[^cite_note-112][^cite_note-113] Trump was discharged from the hospital on October 5.[^cite_note-Hook-114]

Trump being diagnosed with COVID-19 was widely seen as having a negative effect on his campaign and shifted the attention of the public back onto COVID-19, an issue which is generally seen as a liability for Trump, due to his response to the COVID-19 pandemic suffering from low approval ratings.[^cite_note-115][^cite_note-116] Being in quarantine also meant Trump was unable to attend rallies, which were a major part of his campaign. As a result of Trump contracting COVID-19, Biden continued campaigning but temporarily ceased running attack ads against him.[^cite_note-117][^cite_note-118] On October 12, one week after his discharge from the hospital, Trump resumed in-person rallies.[^cite_note-Hook-114] Trump continued to travel to battleground states and hold mass rallies, sometimes two or three in a day. His rallies were criticized for their lack of social distancing or mask wearing, and some polls suggested that voters saw him less favorably for potentially endangering attendees.[^cite_note-119][^cite_note-120]

#### Foreign interference

Further information: [Russian interference in the 2020 United States elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_interference_in_the_2020_United_States_elections) and [Foreign electoral intervention](52846360.md)

U.S. officials accused [Russia](25391.md), [China](5405.md), and [Iran](14653.md) of trying to influence the 2020 United States elections.[^cite_note-121][^cite_note-122] On October 4, 2019, Microsoft announced that "[Phosphorus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charming_Kitten)", a group of hackers linked to the [Iranian government](50913538.md), had attempted to compromise e-mail accounts belonging to journalists, U.S. government officials and the campaign of a U.S. presidential candidate.[^cite_note-123][^cite_note-124] The American Government owned *[Voice of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voice_of_America)* reported in April 2020 that "Internet security researchers say there have already been signs that China-allied hackers have engaged in so-called '[spear-phishing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spear-phishing)' attacks on American political targets ahead of the 2020 vote." Chinese spokesman [Geng Shuang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geng_Shuang) denied the allegations and said he would "hope the people of the U.S. not drag China into its electoral politics".[^cite_note-125]

On February 13, 2020, American intelligence officials advised members of the [House Intelligence Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_Intelligence_Committee) that Russia was interfering in the 2020 election in an effort to get Trump re-elected.[^cite_note-126][^cite_note-127] The briefing was delivered by [Shelby Pierson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shelby_Pierson), the intelligence community's top election security official and an aide to acting [Director of National Intelligence](456082.md) [Joseph Maguire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Maguire). On February 21, *[The Washington Post](102226.md)* reported that, according to unnamed U.S. officials, Russia was interfering in the Democratic primary in an effort to support the nomination of Senator [Bernie Sanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernie_Sanders). Sanders issued a statement after the news report, saying in part, "I don't care, frankly, who Putin wants to be president. My message to Putin is clear: stay out of American elections, and as president, I will make sure that you do."[^cite_note-128] Sanders acknowledged that his campaign was briefed about Russia's alleged efforts about a month prior.[^cite_note-129] In a February 2020 briefing to the House Intelligence Committee, U.S. intelligence officials warned Congress that Russia was interfering in the 2020 campaign to support Trump's reelection campaign; Trump was angered that Congress had been informed of the threat, and the day after the briefing castigated the acting director of national intelligence, Joseph Maguire, for allowing the briefing to go forward.[^cite_note-130][^cite_note-TodayRussiaChina-131] China and some government-linked Chinese individuals have been accused of interfering in the election to support the candidacy of both Biden and Trump,[^cite_note-132] though whether it is actually doing so is disputed among the intelligence community.[^cite_note-TodayRussiaChina-131][^cite_note-133]

On October 21, threatening emails were sent to Democrats in at least four states. The emails warned, "You will vote for Trump on Election Day or we will come after you."[^cite_note-134] [Director of National Intelligence](456082.md) [John Ratcliffe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Ratcliffe_\(American_politician\)) announced that evening that the emails, using a spoofed return address, had been sent by Iran. He added that both Iran and Russia are known to have obtained American voter registration data, possibly from publicly available information, and "This data can be used by foreign actors to attempt to communicate false information to registered voters that they hope will cause confusion, sow chaos and undermine your confidence in American democracy." A spokesman for Iran denied the allegation.[^cite_note-135] In his announcement, Ratcliffe said Iran's intent had been "to intimidate voters, incite social unrest, and damage President Trump", raising questions as to how ordering Democrats to vote for Trump would be damaging to Trump. It was later reported that the reference to Trump had not been in Ratcliffe's prepared remarks as signed off by the other officials on the stage; he had added it on his own.[^cite_note-136] On November 18, 2021, the Justice Department charged two Iranian hackers with attempting to intimidate American voters ahead of the 2020 U.S. election by sending threatening emails and spreading false information.[^cite_note-137]

Throughout the election period, several [Colombian](5222.md) lawmakers and the Colombian ambassador to the United States issued statements supporting the Donald Trump campaign, which has been viewed as potentially harmful to [Colombia–United States relations](4340865.md).[^cite_note-138][^cite_note-139] On October 26, the U.S. Ambassador to Colombia, [Philip Goldberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_Goldberg), requested that Colombian politicians abstain from getting involved in the elections.[^cite_note-140]

The Department of Justice is investigating whether the Trump Victory Committee accepted a \$100,000 donation from [Malaysian](3607937.md) businessman and international fugitive [Jho Low](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jho_Low), who is accused of being the mastermind behind the multibillion-dollar [1Malaysia Development Berhad scandal](47550643.md) involving a Malaysian sovereign wealth fund, [1MDB](25429123.md).[^cite_note-141][^cite_note-142]

Government officials and American corporate security officers braced for a repeat of [2016's election infrastructure hacking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_interference_in_the_2016_United_States_elections#Intrusions_into_state_election_systems) and [similar twenty-first century attacks](21093198.md), and in fact conducted what were characterized as preemptive counter-strikes on [botnet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botnet) infrastructure which might be used in large-scale coordination of hacking,[^cite_note-NYT20201012-143] and [some incidents earlier in the year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_security_hacking_incidents#2020) appeared to foreshadow such possibilities. Nonetheless, after his [dismissal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trump_post-election_dismissals), in a December 2020 interview, [Chris Krebs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Krebs), the Trump administration's director of the [Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency](59407369.md) (CISA), described monitoring Election Day from CISA's joint command center along with representatives from the military's [United States Cyber Command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Cyber_Command), the [National Security Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Security_Agency) (NSA), the [Federal Bureau of Investigation](11127.md) (FBI), the [United States Secret Service](58221.md) (USSS), the [Election Assistance Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_Assistance_Commission) (EAC), representatives of vendors of [voting machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_machine) equipment, and representatives of [state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_governments_of_the_United_States) and [local governments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_the_United_States), as well as his agency's analysis preceding and after that day, saying, "It was quiet. There was no indication or evidence that there was any sort of hacking or compromise of election systems on, before, or after November third."[^cite_note-60Min20201129-144] Responding to spurious claims of foreign outsourcing of [vote counting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vote_counting) as a rationale behind litigation attempting to stop official vote-counting in some areas, Krebs also affirmed that, "All votes in the United States of America are counted in the United States of America."[^cite_note-60Min20201129-144]

Acts of foreign interference included Russian state-directed application of [computational propaganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computational_propaganda) approaches, more conventional [state-sponsored Internet propaganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State-sponsored_Internet_propaganda), [smaller-scale disinformation efforts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_interference_in_the_2020_United_States_elections#Social-media_disinformation_and_voting_infrastructure), "[information laundering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_laundering)" and "[trading up the chain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trading_up_the_chain)" propaganda tactics employing some government officials, [Trump affiliates, and US media outlets](102227.md#Hunter_Biden_laptop_story).[^cite_note-NYT20201025-145]

#### Trump's potential rejection of election results

Main article: [Attempts to overturn the 2020 United States presidential election § Uncertainty over Trump accepting an electoral loss in 2020](65905202.md#Uncertainty_over_Trump_accepting_an_electoral_loss_in_2020)

Further information: [Blue shift (politics)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_shift_\(politics\)) and [Peaceful transition of power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peaceful_transition_of_power)

During the campaign, Trump indicated in [Twitter posts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_on_Twitter), interviews, and speeches that he might refuse to recognize the outcome of the election if he were defeated; Trump falsely suggested that the election would be [rigged](382165.md) against him.[^cite_note-146][^cite_note-147][^cite_note-148] In July 2020, Trump declined to answer whether he would accept the results, just as he did in the [2016 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election), telling Fox News anchor [Chris Wallace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Wallace) that "I have to see. No, I'm not going to just say yes. I'm not going to say no."[^cite_note-149][^cite_note-auto1-150][^cite_note-151][^cite_note-152] Trump repeatedly claimed that "the only way" he could lose would be if the election was "rigged" and repeatedly refused to commit to a [peaceful transition of power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peaceful_transition_of_power) after the election.[^cite_note-153][^cite_note-154] Trump also attacked [mail-in voting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postal_voting_in_the_2020_United_States_elections) throughout the campaign, falsely claiming that the practice contains high rates of fraud;[^cite_note-155][^cite_note-156][^cite_note-157] at one point, Trump said, "We'll see what happens ... Get rid of the ballots and you'll have a very peaceful—there won't be a transfer, frankly. There will be a continuation."[^cite_note-158] Trump's statements have been described as a threat "to upend the constitutional order".[^cite_note-159] In September 2020, FBI Director [Christopher A. Wray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_A._Wray), who was appointed by Trump, testified under oath that the FBI has "not seen, historically, any kind of coordinated national voter fraud effort in a major election, whether it's by mail or otherwise".[^cite_note-160]

A number of congressional Republicans insisted they were committed to an orderly and peaceful transition of power, but declined to criticize Trump for his comments.[^cite_note-161] On September 24, the Senate unanimously passed a resolution affirming the Senate's commitment to a peaceful transfer of power.[^cite_note-162] Trump also stated he expected the [Supreme Court](31737.md) to decide the election and that he wanted a conservative majority in case of an election dispute, reiterating his commitment to quickly install a [ninth justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_Supreme_Court_vacancy) following the death of [Ruth Bader Ginsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruth_Bader_Ginsburg).[^cite_note-163]

#### Election delay suggestion

In April 2020, Biden predicted that Trump would try to delay the election, saying he "is gonna try to kick back the election somehow, come up with some rationale why it can't be held".[^cite_note-164][^cite_note-165] In May, [Jared Kushner](6191053.md) did not rule out delaying the election, saying "I'm not sure I can commit one way or the other".[^cite_note-166] On July 30, Trump tweeted that "With Universal Mail-In Voting (not Absentee Voting, which is good), 2020 will be the most INACCURATE & FRAUDULENT Election in history" and proposed that the election should be delayed. Asked whether Trump had the relevant authority, [Secretary of State](32293.md) [Mike Pompeo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Pompeo) said "the [United States Department of Justice](52563.md) will make that determination."[^cite_note-167] However, only Congress has the power to schedule elections,[^cite_note-168][^cite_note-169] and the Constitution sets the end of the presidential and vice-presidential terms at January 20, a hard deadline which can only be altered by constitutional amendment.[^cite_note-170][^cite_note-171] Congress refused to consider Trump's proposal,[^cite_note-172][^cite_note-173] and the election went ahead as scheduled.

#### Postal voting

See also: [Expansion of postal voting in the 2020 United States elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postal_voting_in_the_United_States#Expansion_in_2020_election) and [2020 United States Postal Service crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_Postal_Service_crisis)



Chart of July 2020 opinion survey on likelihood of voting by mail in November election, compared to 2016

[^cite_note-174]

[Postal voting in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postal_voting_in_the_United_States) has become increasingly common, with 25% of voters mailing their ballots in 2016 and 2018. By June 2020, the [COVID-19 pandemic](63136490.md) was predicted to cause a large increase in mail voting because of the possible danger of congregating at polling places.[^cite_note-175] An August 2020 state-by-state analysis concluded that 76% of Americans were eligible to vote by mail in 2020, a record number. The analysis predicted that 80 million ballots could be cast by mail in 2020—more than double the number in 2016.[^cite_note-176]

The Postal Service sent a letter to multiple states in July 2020, warning that the service would not be able to meet the state's deadlines for requesting and casting last-minute absentee ballots.[^cite_note-177] In addition to the anticipated high volume of mailed ballots, the prediction was due in part to numerous measures taken by [Louis DeJoy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_DeJoy), the newly installed [United States Postmaster General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Postmaster_General), including banning overtime and extra trips to deliver mail,[^cite_note-178] which caused delays in delivering mail,[^cite_note-179] and dismantling and removing hundreds of high-speed mail sorting machines from postal centers.[^cite_note-180] On August 18, after the House of Representatives had been recalled from its August break to vote on a bill reversing the changes, DeJoy announced that he would roll back all the changes until after the November election. He said he would reinstate overtime hours, roll back service reductions, and halt the removal of mail-sorting machines and collection boxes.[^cite_note-181]

The House of Representatives voted an emergency grant of \$25 billion to the post office to facilitate the predicted flood of mail ballots.[^cite_note-182] Although Trump has repeatedly denounced mail voting, he has mailed in ballots due to being in a different state than the one where he votes at the time of the election.[^cite_note-183] In August 2020, Trump conceded that the post office would need additional funds to handle the additional mail-in voting, but said he would block any additional funding for the post office to prevent any increase in balloting by mail.[^cite_note-184]

In the end, an estimated 42 percent of votes were cast by mail across 41 reporting states,[^cite_note-185] approximately 65.6 million ballots out of 154.6 million cast by all methods.[^cite_note-EarlyVote2020-11]

The Trump campaign filed lawsuits seeking to block the use of official ballot dropboxes in Pennsylvania in locations other than an election office, and also sought to "block election officials from counting mail-in ballots if a voter forgets to put their mail-in ballot in a secrecy sleeve within the ballot return-envelope".[^cite_note-Levine-186] The Trump campaign and the Republican Party both failed to produce any evidence of vote-by-mail fraud after being ordered by a federal judge to do so.[^cite_note-Levine-186]

On Election Day, a judge ordered mail inspectors to search "mail facilities in .... key battleground states" for ballots.[^cite_note-187] The agency refused to comply with the order and nearly 7% of ballots in [USPS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USPS) facilities on Election Day were not processed in time.[^cite_note-188]

#### Federal Election Commission issues

The [Federal Election Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Election_Commission), which was created in 1974 to enforce [campaign finance laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campaign_finance_in_the_United_States) in federal elections, has not functioned since July 2020 due to vacancies in membership. In the absence of a quorum, the commission cannot vote on complaints or give guidance through advisory opinions.[^cite_note-189] As of May 19, 2020, there were 350 outstanding matters on the agency's enforcement docket and 227 items waiting for action.[^cite_note-190] As of September 1, 2020, Trump had not nominated anyone to fill the FEC vacancies.[^cite_note-191]

#### Supreme Court vacancy

Further information: [Amy Coney Barrett Supreme Court nomination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amy_Coney_Barrett_Supreme_Court_nomination) and [Death and state funeral of Ruth Bader Ginsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_Ruth_Bader_Ginsburg)



President Donald Trump with [Amy Coney Barrett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amy_Coney_Barrett) and her family, just prior to Barrett being announced as the nominee, September 26, 2020

On September 18, 2020, Justice [Ruth Bader Ginsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruth_Bader_Ginsburg) died. [Senate Majority Leader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_Majority_Leader) [Mitch McConnell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitch_McConnell) immediately said the [precedent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precedent) he had set regarding the [Merrick Garland nomination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merrick_Garland_Supreme_Court_nomination) was inoperative and that a replacement would be voted on as soon as possible, setting the stage for a confirmation battle and an unexpected intrusion into the campaign.[^cite_note-192] The death of Justice Ginsburg resulted in large increases in momentum for both the Democrats and Republicans.[^cite_note-193][^cite_note-194] The president,[^cite_note-195] vice president,[^cite_note-196] and several Republican members of Congress said a full Supreme Court bench was needed to decide the upcoming election.[^cite_note-197][^cite_note-198]

On September 26, the day after Justice Ginsburg's body lay in state at the [Capitol](31979.md), Trump held a Rose Garden ceremony at the White House to announce and introduce his candidate, [Amy Coney Barrett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amy_Coney_Barrett).[^cite_note-199] After four days of confirmation hearings, the Senate Judiciary Committee voted the nomination out of committee on October 22,[^cite_note-NPRBarrett-200] and on October 26, Barrett was confirmed on a [party-line vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party-line_vote) of 52–48, with no Democrats voting for her confirmation.[^cite_note-Mascaro-201] This was the closest Supreme Court confirmation ever to a presidential election, and the first Supreme Court nomination since 1869 with no supporting votes from the minority party.[^cite_note-Mascaro-201] It was also one of the fastest timelines from nomination to confirmations in U.S. history.[^cite_note-fastest-202][^cite_note-NPRBarrett-200]

#### Pre-election litigation

Main article: [Pre-election lawsuits related to the 2020 U.S. presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-election_lawsuits_related_to_the_2020_U.S._presidential_election)

By September 2020, several hundred legal cases relating to the election had been filed.[^cite_note-NPRVoting-203] About 250 of these had to do with the mechanics of voting in relation to the COVID-19 pandemic.[^cite_note-NPRVoting-203] The Supreme Court ruled on a number of these cases,[^cite_note-204] primarily issuing emergency stays instead of going through the normal process due to the urgency.[^cite_note-205] In October 2020, there was speculation that the election might be decided through a Supreme Court case, as happened following the [2000 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_United_States_presidential_election).[^cite_note-206][^cite_note-207]

### Debates

Main article: [2020 United States presidential debates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_debates)

Map of United States showing debate locations

Case Western Reserve University

**[Case Western Reserve University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Case_Western_Reserve_University)**

University of Utah

**[University of Utah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Utah)**

Belmont University

**[Belmont University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belmont_University)**

Sites of the 2020 general election presidential () and vice presidential () debates

On October 11, 2019, the [Commission on Presidential Debates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commission_on_Presidential_Debates) (CPD) announced that three general election debates would be held in the fall of 2020.[^cite_note-208]

The first, moderated by [Chris Wallace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Wallace), took place on September 29, and was co-hosted by [Case Western Reserve University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Case_Western_Reserve_University) and the [Cleveland Clinic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleveland_Clinic) in [Cleveland, Ohio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleveland,_Ohio).[^cite_note-AxiosDebate-209] The debate was originally to be hosted at the [University of Notre Dame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Notre_Dame) in [Notre Dame, Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Notre_Dame,_Indiana), but the university decided against holding the debate as a result of the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Indiana).[^cite_note-AxiosDebate-209][^cite_note-210] Biden was generally held to have won the first debate,[^cite_note-211][^cite_note-212][^cite_note-213] with a significant minority of commentators deeming it a draw.[^cite_note-214][^cite_note-215]

One exchange that was particularly noted was when Trump did not directly denounce the [white supremacist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_supremacist) and [neo-fascist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-fascist) group [Proud Boys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proud_Boys), instead responding that they should "stand back and stand by".[^cite_note-216][^cite_note-217][^cite_note-218] On the next day, Trump told reporters the group should "stand down" while also claiming that he was not aware of what the group was.[^cite_note-219][^cite_note-220] The debate was described as "chaotic and nearly incoherent" because of Trump's repeated interruptions, causing the CPD to consider adjustments to the format of the remaining debates.[^cite_note-221]

The vice presidential debate was held on October 7, 2020, at the [University of Utah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Utah) in [Salt Lake City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salt_Lake_City).[^cite_note-222] The debate was widely held to be subdued, with no clear victor.[^cite_note-223][^cite_note-224] One incident that was particularly commented on was when a [fly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fly) landed on vice-president Pence's head, and remained there unbeknownst to him for two minutes.[^cite_note-225][^cite_note-226]

The second debate was initially set to be held at the [University of Michigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Michigan) in [Ann Arbor, Michigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ann_Arbor,_Michigan), but the university withdrew in June 2020, over concerns regarding the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Michigan).[^cite_note-227] The planned debate was rescheduled for October 15 at the [Adrienne Arsht Center for the Performing Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adrienne_Arsht_Center_for_the_Performing_Arts) in [Miami](53846.md); due to Trump contracting COVID-19, the CPD announced on October 8 that the debate would be held virtually, in which the candidates would appear from separate locations. Trump refused to participate in a virtual debate, and the commission subsequently announced that the debate had been cancelled.[^cite_note-228][^cite_note-229]

The third scheduled debate took place on October 22 at [Belmont University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belmont_University) in [Nashville, Tennessee](22018.md), and was moderated by [Kristen Welker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kristen_Welker).[^cite_note-230][^cite_note-231] The changes to the debate rules, which included the candidates' microphones being muted while the other was speaking,[^cite_note-232] resulted in it being generally considered more civil than the first debate.[^cite_note-233] Welker's performance as moderator was praised, with her being regarded as having done a good job preventing the candidates from interrupting each other.[^cite_note-234] Biden was generally held to have won the debate, though it was considered unlikely to alter the race to any considerable degree.[^cite_note-235][^cite_note-236][^cite_note-237]

### Endorsements

Main articles: [List of Donald Trump 2020 presidential campaign endorsements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Donald_Trump_2020_presidential_campaign_endorsements), [List of Joe Biden 2020 presidential campaign endorsements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Joe_Biden_2020_presidential_campaign_endorsements), [List of Jo Jorgensen 2020 presidential campaign endorsements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Jo_Jorgensen_2020_presidential_campaign_endorsements), and [List of Howie Hawkins 2020 presidential campaign endorsements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Howie_Hawkins_2020_presidential_campaign_endorsements)

### Total cost estimate

[OpenSecrets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OpenSecrets) estimated the total cost of the 2020 election nearly \$14 billion, making it the most expensive election in history and twice as expensive as the previous presidential election cycle.[^cite_note-265]

## Campaign issues

### COVID-19 pandemic

Further information: [COVID-19 pandemic in the United States](63136490.md), [U.S. federal government response to the COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._federal_government_response_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic), and [Communication of the Trump administration during the COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communication_of_the_Trump_administration_during_the_COVID-19_pandemic)

The [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md) was a major issue of the campaign, with Trump's responses being heavily criticized. The president spread mixed messages on the value of wearing face masks as protection, including criticizing Biden and reporters for wearing them, but has also encouraged their use at times.[^cite_note-266] During the campaign, Trump held many events across the country, including in COVID-19 hotspots, where attendees did not wear masks and were not socially distancing; at the same time, he mocked those who wore face masks.[^cite_note-267][^cite_note-268][^cite_note-269]

Biden advocated for the expansion of federal funding, including funding under the [Defense Production Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defense_Production_Act) for testing, personal protective equipment, and research.[^cite_note-moore-pandemic-plans-270] Trump also invoked the Defense Production Act to control the distribution of masks and ventilators,[^cite_note-271] but his response plan relied significantly on a vaccine being released by the end of 2020.[^cite_note-moore-pandemic-plans-270] At the second presidential debate, Trump claimed Biden had called him xenophobic for restricting entry from foreign nationals who had visited China, but Biden responded that he had not been referring to this decision.[^cite_note-272]

### Economy



Proposed tax plan payment rates by income group as a percentage of income, including mandatory health insurance

Main articles: [Economic policy of the first Trump administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_policy_of_the_first_Trump_administration) and [Economic policies of Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_positions_of_Joe_Biden#Economic_issues)

Trump claimed credit for the consistent economic expansion of his presidency's first three years, with the stock market at its longest growth period in history and unemployment at a fifty-year low. Additionally, he has touted the 2020 third-quarter rebound, in which GDP grew at an annualized rate of 33.1%, as evidence of the success of his economic policies.[^cite_note-273] Biden responded to Trump's claims by repeating that the strong economy under Trump's presidency was inherited from the Obama administration, and that Trump has aggravated the economic impact of the pandemic, including the need for 42 million Americans to file for unemployment.[^cite_note-274]

The [Tax Cuts and Jobs Act of 2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax_Cuts_and_Jobs_Act_of_2017), which lowered [income taxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_tax_in_the_United_States) for many Americans and lowered the [corporate tax rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corporate_tax_in_the_United_States) from 35% to 21%, were a major component of Trump's economic policy. Biden and the Democrats generally describe these cuts as unfairly benefiting the upper class. Biden plans to raise taxes on corporations and those making over \$400,000 per year, while keeping the reduced taxes on lower-income brackets and raise [capital gains taxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_gains_tax) to a maximum bracket of 39.6%. In response, Trump said Biden's plans would destroy [retirement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Retirement) accounts and the [stock market](52328.md).[^cite_note-275]

### Environment

Main articles: [Environmental policy of the first Trump administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_policy_of_the_first_Trump_administration), [Environmental policy of Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_positions_of_Joe_Biden#Environment_and_climate_change), and [Climate change policy of the United States](25447994.md)

Trump and Biden's views on environmental policy differ significantly. Trump stated that [climate change](5042951.md) is a hoax, although he also called it a serious subject.[^cite_note-276] Trump condemned the [Paris Agreement](30242372.md) on [greenhouse gas reduction](2119179.md) and began [the withdrawal process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_and_the_Paris_Agreement). Biden planned to rejoin it and announced a \$2 trillion plan to combat climate change. Biden had not fully accepted the [Green New Deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_New_Deal). Biden did not plan to ban [fracking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fracking) but rather to outlaw new fracking on federal land. In a debate, Trump claimed Biden wanted to ban it altogether. Trump's other environmental policies included the removal of [methane emission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methane_emissions) standards, and an expansion of mining.[^cite_note-277]

### Health care

Main article: [Health care in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_care_in_the_United_States)

Health care was a divisive issue in both the Democratic primary campaign and the general campaign. While Biden, as well as other candidates, promised protection of the [Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affordable_Care_Act), progressives within the Democratic Party advocated to replace the private insurance industry with [Medicare for All](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_for_All). Biden's plan involves adding a [public option](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_health_insurance_option) to the American healthcare system,[^cite_note-278] and the restoration of the [individual mandate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_insurance_mandate) to buy health care, which was removed from the Affordable Care Act by the 2017 tax cut bill,[^cite_note-trump-dominate-279] as well as restoring funding for [Planned Parenthood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planned_Parenthood). Trump announced plans to repeal the Affordable Care Act, calling it "too expensive", but he did not say what would replace it.[^cite_note-280] At the time of the election, the Trump administration and Republican officials from 18 states had a lawsuit before the Supreme Court, asking the court to repeal the Affordable Care Act.[^cite_note-281]

### Racial unrest

Main article: [2020–2021 United States racial unrest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020%E2%80%932021_United_States_racial_unrest)



[George Floyd protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Floyd_protests) in [Minneapolis](6097240.md) on May 26

As a result of the [murder of George Floyd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murder_of_George_Floyd) and other incidents of [police brutality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police_brutality) against African Americans, combined with the effects of the COVID-19 pandemic, a series of protests and a wider [period of racial unrest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020%E2%80%932021_United_States_racial_unrest) erupted in mid-2020. This was followed by the [Black Lives Matter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Lives_Matter) movement, which protested police brutality against [black people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_people), and became a central point of the 2020 presidential campaign.[^cite_note-282] Protests were mostly peaceful; fewer than 4% involved property damage or violence (with most of the latter directed at BLM protesters themselves).[^cite_note-283] According to a September 2020 estimate, arson, vandalism, and looting caused about \$1–2 billion in insured damage between May 26 and June 8, making this initial phase of the [George Floyd protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Floyd_protests) the civil disorder event with the highest recorded damage in United States history.[^cite_note-Riot_damage-284][^cite_note-285]

In response, Trump and the Republicans suggested sending in the military to counter the protests, which was criticized, especially by Democrats, as heavy-handed and potentially illegal.[^cite_note-286] Trump referred to Black Lives Matter protesters confronting diners in a restaurant as "thugs",[^cite_note-287] and called a street painting of the slogan a "symbol of hate".[^cite_note-288] Particularly controversial was [a photo-op Trump took in front of St. John's Church in Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_photo_op_at_St._John's_Church), before which military police had forcefully cleared peaceful protestors from the area.[^cite_note-trump-dominate-279] Biden condemned Trump for his actions against protestors; he described George Floyd's words "[I can't breathe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I_can't_breathe)" as a "wake-up call for our nation". He also promised he would create a police oversight commission in his first 100 days as president, and establish a uniform use of force standard, as well as other police reform measures.[^cite_note-289]

## Timeline

## Results



Early voting in [Cleveland, Ohio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleveland,_Ohio)

See also: [Attempts to overturn the 2020 United States presidential election](65905202.md)

### Statistics



In 14 states, representing 223 electoral votes, a plurality of voters chose to [stay home](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abstention) rather than vote for any candidate.

[^cite_note-291]

More than 158 million votes were cast in the election.[^cite_note-292] More than 100 million of them were cast before Election Day by [early voting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_voting) or [mail ballot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postal_voting), due to the ongoing [COVID-19 pandemic](63136490.md).[^cite_note-293] The election saw the highest [voter turnout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter_turnout_in_United_States_presidential_elections) as a percentage of eligible voters since 1900,[^cite_note-294] with each of the two main tickets receiving more than 74 million votes, surpassing [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama)'s record of 69.5 million votes in [2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_United_States_presidential_election).[^cite_note-\:6-295] The Biden–Harris ticket received more than 81 million votes, the most votes ever in a [U.S. presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_presidential_candidates_by_number_of_votes_received).[^cite_note-\:7-13][^cite_note-\:8-14] It was also the ninth consecutive presidential election where the victorious [major party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two-party_system#United_States) nominee did not receive a [popular vote majority by a double-digit margin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_presidential_elections_by_popular_vote_margin) over the losing major party nominee(s), continuing the longest sequence of such presidential elections in U.S. history, which began in [1988](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_United_States_presidential_election) and in [2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election) eclipsed the previous longest sequence, that from [1876](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1876_United_States_presidential_election) through [1900](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1900_United_States_presidential_election).[^cite_note-296][^cite_note-297][^cite_note-Williams_2012_pp._1539–1570-298] In 2020, 58 percent of U.S. voters lived in [landslide counties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_states_and_blue_states#Polarization),[^cite_note-299] a decline from 61 percent in 2016.[^cite_note-300]

Trump became the 11th incumbent in the country's history, and the first since [1992](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_United_States_presidential_election), to lose a bid for a second term. Biden's 51.3% of the popular vote was the highest for a challenger to an incumbent president since [1932](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1932_United_States_presidential_election).[^cite_note-\:3-301][^cite_note-\:4-302][^cite_note-\:5-303][^cite_note-304] Biden is the sixth vice president to become president without [succeeding to the office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_presidential_line_of_succession) on the death or resignation of a previous president.[^cite_note-Azari-305] Additionally, Trump's loss marked the third time an elected president lost the popular vote twice, the first being [John Quincy Adams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Quincy_Adams) in the 1820s and [Benjamin Harrison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Harrison) in the 1880s and 1890s.[^cite_note-CNN.Led-306] This was the first time since [1980](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_United_States_presidential_election), and the first for Republicans since [1892](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1892_United_States_presidential_election) that a party was voted out after a single four-year term. This was the second election in American history in which the incumbent president lost re-election despite winning a greater share of the popular vote than he did in the previous election, after 1828. It is also the third election in which the two candidates that received electoral votes carried the same number of states. This also happened in [1880](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1880_United_States_presidential_election) and [1848](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1848_United_States_presidential_election).

Biden won 25 states, the District of Columbia, and one congressional district in Nebraska, totaling 306 electoral votes. Trump won 25 states and one congressional district in Maine, totaling 232 electoral votes. This result was exactly the reverse of Trump's victory, 306 to 232, in [2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election) (excluding [faithless electors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faithless_elector)).[^cite_note-307] Biden became the first Democrat to win the presidential election in [Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election_in_Georgia) since [1992](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_United_States_presidential_election)[^cite_note-autogenerated1-308] and in [Arizona](59561924.md) since [1996](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_United_States_presidential_election), and the first candidate to win nationally without [Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election_in_Florida) since 1992 and [Ohio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election_in_Ohio) since [1960](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1960_United_States_presidential_election), casting doubt on Ohio's continued status as a [bellwether state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bellwether_state).[^cite_note-cincinnati1960-309] Biden also became the first Democrat to win the presidential election without [Iowa](26810748.md) since [1976](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1976_United_States_presidential_election).[^cite_note-310] Biden carried five states won by Trump in 2016: Arizona, [Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election_in_Georgia), [Michigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election_in_Michigan), [Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election_in_Pennsylvania), and [Wisconsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election_in_Wisconsin). Despite his relatively comfortable 74 vote margin in the Electoral College, Biden only won the decisive states of Wisconsin, Georgia and Arizona by a combined 43,000 votes. He also became the first Democrat since [2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_United_States_presidential_election_in_Nebraska) to carry [Nebraska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election_in_Nebraska)'s [2nd congressional district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nebraska's_2nd_congressional_district), winning one electoral vote from the state.[^cite_note-311] Trump did not win any states won by Clinton in 2016. Biden's three gains in the [Rust Belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rust_Belt)—Michigan, Pennsylvania and Wisconsin—were widely characterized as a rebuilding of the [blue wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_wall_\(United_States\)), a term widely used in the press for the states consistently won by Democrats from 1992 to 2012, broken by Trump in 2016 when he narrowly flipped those three Rust Belt states.[^cite_note-312][^cite_note-313][^cite_note-314][^cite_note-315] Nevertheless, amidst Trump's national and electoral defeat, his scoring decisive victories in Ohio, Iowa, and Florida for the second time, after their having backed Obama twice, has led many commentators to conclude they have shifted from perennial [swing states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swing_state) to reliable [red states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_states_and_blue_states).[^cite_note-316][^cite_note-317]

If Biden's three narrowest state victories—Wisconsin, Georgia, and Arizona, all of which he won by less than a percentage point—had gone to Trump, there would have been a tie of 269 electors for each candidate,[^cite_note-tippingpointstate-318][^cite_note-Skelley-538-319] causing a [contingent election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contingent_election) to be decided by the House of Representatives, where Trump had the advantage. (Even though Democrats controlled the House, contingent elections are determined by state delegations in which each state receives just one vote, and since a slight majority of states in 2020 contained more Republican than Democratic representatives, Republicans would have had more votes in such an election.) This scenario would have required a popular-vote shift of 0.63% or less in each of these three states, a total of about 43,000 votes, 0.03% of votes cast nationally.[^cite_note-320] This situation paralleled 2016, when a shift of 0.77% or less in each of the three most closely contested states (Wisconsin, Michigan, and Pennsylvania), or about 77,000 votes, would have resulted in the popular vote winner Hillary Clinton also winning in the Electoral College.

This was the first time since 1948 that Democrats won the popular vote in four elections in a row. Biden was the second former vice president (after [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon) in [1968](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1968_United_States_Presidential_election)) to win the presidency, as well as the first vice president since [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush) in 1988 to be elected president.[^cite_note-321]

Almost all counties previously considered reliable indicators of eventual success in presidential elections voted for Trump instead of Biden, meaning that they did not continue their streaks as [bellwether counties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_election_bellwether_counties_in_the_United_States). This was attributed to increasing political polarization throughout the country and to the urban-rural divide.[^cite_note-wiped-322]

While Trump still dominated rural America as a whole, there were rural areas that he lost. Biden won 50.5% of the rural counties that each had mostly non-white voters, particularly in the South and the West.[^cite_note-\:14-323] Rural counties in the South won by Biden had greater economic distress than those won by Trump; in the Northeast, the opposite was true.[^cite_note-\:14-323] In the West, Biden did especially well in rural counties that had high shares of workers employed in leisure and hospitality.[^cite_note-\:14-323] Such counties likewise had large constituencies of immigration from other states.[^cite_note-\:14-323] Biden was the [oldest person](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_presidents_of_the_United_States_by_age) to win a presidential election, at age 77 surpassing Trump's 2016 record of doing so at age 70. Biden was also the oldest president ever elected, surpassing Ronald Reagan's 1984 record of doing so at age 73. Four years later, in 2024, Trump surpassed both records of doing so at age 78. Of the 3,153 counties/districts/independent cities making returns, Trump won the most popular votes in 2,595 (82.30%) while Biden carried 558 (17.70%).

### Election calls

Major news organizations project a state for a candidate when there is high mathematical confidence that the outstanding vote would be unlikely to prevent the projected winner from ultimately winning the state. Election projections are made by decision teams of political scientists and data scientists.[^cite_note-math-19]



People celebrate in the streets [near the White House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Lives_Matter_Plaza) after the major networks projected Biden the winner of the election on November 7.



Senator [Chuck Schumer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chuck_Schumer) addresses a crowd celebrating in [Times Square](31389.md), [New York City](645042.md), shortly after the election was called for Biden.

In the early hours of November 4, the Associated Press called Florida for Donald Trump at 12\:35 a.m. EST, putting him at 164 electoral votes. By 1\:06 a.m. EST, they called Texas for Trump, putting him at 202 electoral votes, compared to Joe Biden's 224. Arizona and Maine were called for Biden at 2\:51 a.m. and 3\:06 a.m. EST, respectively. At 1\:24 p.m. EST that afternoon, the Associated Press called Maine's 2nd congressional district for Trump, giving him 203 electoral votes. Then, Biden won Wisconsin and Michigan, at 2\:16 p.m. and 3\:58 p.m. EST, respectively, bringing him to 264 electoral votes, just 6 short of the presidency. On the morning of November 7 at approximately 11\:25 a.m. EST, roughly three and a half days after polls had closed, [ABC News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ABC_News_\(United_States\)), [NBC News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBC_News), [CBS News](359551.md), the [Associated Press](18935732.md), [CNN](62028.md), and [Fox News](11121.md) all called the election and Pennsylvania's 20 electoral votes for Biden, based on projections of votes in Pennsylvania showing him leading outside of the recount threshold (0.5% in that state), placing him at 284 electoral votes.[^cite_note-324][^cite_note-325][^cite_note-326][^cite_note-327][^cite_note-328][^cite_note-329][^cite_note-330] That evening, Biden and Harris gave their victory speeches in Wilmington, Delaware.[^cite_note-331]

### OSCE election monitoring

On the invitation of the U.S. State Department, the [Organization for Security and Co-operation in Europe](22796.md)'s (OSCE) [Office for Democratic Institutions and Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_for_Democratic_Institutions_and_Human_Rights) (ODIHR), which has been monitoring U.S. elections since 2002 (as it does for major elections in all other OSCE member countries), sent 102 observers from 39 countries.[^cite_note-\:02-332][^cite_note-\:12-333][^cite_note-\:2-334] The task force consisted of long-term observers from the ODIHR office (led by former Polish diplomat [Urszula Gacek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urszula_Gacek)) deployed to 28 states from September on and covering 15 states on election day, and a group of European lawmakers acting as short-term observers (led by German parliamentarian [Michael Georg Link](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Georg_Link)), reporting from Maryland, Virginia, California, Nevada, Michigan, Missouri, Wisconsin, and D.C.[^cite_note-\:02-332][^cite_note-\:2-334] Due to the COVID-19 pandemic, it was scaled down to a "limited election observation mission" from the originally planned 100 long-term observers and 400 short-term observers.[^cite_note-\:02-332]

An interim report published by the OSCE shortly before the election noted that many ODIHR interlocutors "expressed grave concerns about the risk of legitimacy of the elections being questioned due to the incumbent President's repeated allegations of a fraudulent election process, and postal vote in particular."[^cite_note-\:02-332][^cite_note-335] On the day after the election, the task force published preliminary findings,[^cite_note-\:12-333] with part of the summary stating:

> The 3 November general elections were competitive and well managed despite legal uncertainties and logistical challenges. In a highly polarized political environment, harsh campaign rhetoric fuelled tensions. Measures intended to secure the elections during the pandemic triggered protracted litigation driven by partisan interests. The uncertainty caused by late legal challenges and evidence-deficient claims about election fraud created confusion and concern among election officials and voters. Voter registration and identification rules in some states are unduly restrictive for certain groups of citizens. The media, although sharply polarized, provided comprehensive coverage of the campaign and made efforts to provide accurate information on the organization of elections.[^cite_note-336]

Link said that "on the election day itself, we couldn't see any violations" at the polling places visited by the observers.[^cite_note-\:12-333] The task force also found "nothing untoward" while observing the handling of mail-in ballots at post offices, with Gacek being quoted as saying: "We feel that allegations of systemic wrongdoing in these elections have no solid ground. The system has held up well."[^cite_note-\:2-334] The OSCE's election monitoring branch published a more comprehensive report in early 2021.[^cite_note-\:2-334][^cite_note-337]

Two states, Maine and Nebraska, allow their electoral votes to be split between candidates by congressional districts. The winner of each congressional district gets one electoral vote for the district. The winner of the statewide vote gets two additional electoral votes.[^cite_note-349][^cite_note-350]

- [Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_\(US_state\))

### Close states

States where the margin of victory was under 1% (37 electoral votes; all won by Biden):

1. **Georgia, 0.23% (11,779 votes) – 16 electoral votes**

2. **Arizona, 0.30% (10,457 votes) – 11 electoral votes**

3. **Wisconsin, 0.63% (20,682 votes) – 10 electoral votes** ([tipping-point state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tipping-point_state) for Biden victory)

   [^cite_note-tippingpointstate-318]

States where the margin of victory was between 1% and 5% (86 electoral votes; 42 won by Biden, 44 by Trump):

1. **Pennsylvania, 1.16% (80,555 votes) – 20 electoral votes** (tipping-point state for Trump victory)**[^cite_note-Skelley-538-319]**

2. **North Carolina, 1.34% (74,483 votes) – 15 electoral votes**

3. **Nevada, 2.39% (33,596 votes) – 6 electoral votes**

4. **Michigan, 2.78% (154,188 votes) – 16 electoral votes**

5. **Florida, 3.36% (371,686 votes) – 29 electoral votes**

States/districts where the margin of victory was between 5% and 10% (80 electoral votes; 17 won by Biden, 63 by Trump):

1. **Texas, 5.58% (631,221 votes) – 38 electoral votes**

2. **Nebraska's 2nd congressional district, 6.50% (22,091 votes) – 1 electoral vote**

3. **Minnesota, 7.12% (233,012 votes) – 10 electoral votes**

4. **New Hampshire, 7.35% (59,267 votes) – 4 electoral votes**

5. **Maine's 2nd congressional district, 7.44% (27,996 votes) – 1 electoral vote**

6. **Ohio, 8.03% (475,669 votes) – 18 electoral votes**

7. **Iowa, 8.20% (138,611 votes) – 6 electoral votes**

8. **Maine, 9.07% (74,335 votes) – 2 electoral votes**

Blue denotes states or congressional districts won by Democrat Joe Biden; red denotes those won by Republican Donald Trump.
