The [Brookings Institution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brookings_Institution) released a report entitled "Exit polls show both familiar and new voting blocs sealed Biden's win" on November 12, 2020. In it, author [William H. Frey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_H._Frey) attributes Obama's 2008 win to young people, people of color, and the college-educated. Frey contends Trump won in 2016 thanks to older White without college degrees.[^cite_note-frey-355] Frey says the same coalitions largely held in 2008 and 2016, although in key battleground states Biden increased his vote among some of the 2016 Trump groups, particularly among White and older Americans.[^cite_note-frey-355] Trump won the white vote in 2016 by 20% but in 2020 by only 16%. The Democratic Party won black voters by 75%, the lowest margin since [1980](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_United_States_presidential_election). Democrats won the Latino vote by 32%, which is the smallest margin since 2004, and they won the Asian American vote by 27%, the lowest figure since 2008.[^cite_note-frey-355] Biden reduced the Republican margin of white men without college educations from 48% to 42%, and the Democrats made a slight improvement of 2% among white, college-educated women. People age 18 to 29 registered a rise in Democratic support between 2016 and 2020, with the Democratic margin of victory among that demographic increasing from 19% to 24%.[^cite_note-frey-355] However, [Baby boomers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baby_boomers) and [Generation X](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generation_X) still constituted the majority of the voting public. The exit poll estimated that voters 40 years of age or older (born in 1980 or earlier to comprise 68% of the electorate. Voters from age 40 through age 64 (born from 1956 through 1980) alone were estimated to comprise 46% of the electorate.[^cite_note-auto3-290]

Post-election analysis using verified voter data found the [Associated Press](18935732.md)'s Votecast was more accurate than the exit polls.[^cite_note-356][^cite_note-357]

### Voting patterns by ethnicity

#### Hispanic and Latino voters

Biden won 65% of the Latino vote according to [Edison Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edison_Research?action=edit\&redlink=1), and 63% according to the Associated Press. [Voto Latino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voto_Latino) reported that the Latino vote was crucial to the Biden victory in [Arizona](59561924.md). 40% of Latino voters who voted in 2020 did not vote in 2016, and 73% of those Latino voters voted for Biden (438,000 voters).[^cite_note-358] [Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election_in_Florida) and [Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election_in_Texas), which have large Latino populations, were carried by Trump. In Florida, Trump won a majority of [Cuban American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_American) voters in [Miami-Dade County, Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami-Dade_County,_Florida).[^cite_note-latino-359] The Latino vote was still crucial to enable Biden to carry states such as Nevada.[^cite_note-360] Latino voters were targeted by a major Spanish-language [disinformation](142690.md) campaign in the final weeks of the election, with various falsehoods and conspiracy theories being pushed out by [WhatsApp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WhatsApp) and [viral social media posts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_media_in_the_2020_United_States_presidential_election).[^cite_note-361][^cite_note-362][^cite_note-363]

Demographic patterns emerged having to do with [country of origin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Country_of_origin) and candidate preference. Pre- and post-election surveys showed Biden winning Latinos of [Mexican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Americans), [Puerto Rican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerto_Ricans), [Dominican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominican_Americans),[^cite_note-364] [Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Americans), and [Nuevomexicano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanos_of_New_Mexico) heritage,[^cite_note-365] while Trump carried Latinos of Cuban heritage. Data from Florida showed Biden holding a narrow edge among [South Americans](26769.md).[^cite_note-366]

#### Black voters

Biden won 87% of the Black vote, while Trump won 12%.[^cite_note-exit-354] Biden's advantage among Black voters was crucial in the large cities of [Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election_in_Pennsylvania) and [Michigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election_in_Michigan); the increase in the Democratic vote in [Milwaukee County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee_County) of about 28,000 votes was more than the 20,000-vote lead Biden had in the state of [Wisconsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election_in_Wisconsin). Almost half Biden's gains [in Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election_in_Georgia) came from the four largest counties—Fulton, DeKalb, Gwinnett, and Cobb—all in the Atlanta metro area with large Black populations.[^cite_note-367] Trump improved his overall share of the Black vote from 2016 by 4% and doubled the Black vote that [Mitt Romney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitt_Romney) received in 2012.[^cite_note-368][^cite_note-369][^cite_note-exit-354]

#### Asian American and Pacific Island voters

Polls showed that 68% of Asian American and Pacific Island (AAPI) voters supported Biden/Harris, while 28% supported Trump/Pence. [Karthick Ramakrishnan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karthick_Ramakrishnan), a political science professor at the University of California Riverside and founder of AAPI Data, said Asian Americans supported Biden over Trump by about a 2\:1 margin. [Korean Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Americans), [Japanese Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_Americans), [Indian Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Americans), and [Chinese Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Americans) favored Biden by higher margins overall compared to [Vietnamese Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnamese_Americans) and [Filipino Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_Americans).[^cite_note-\:13-370] Many voters were turned off by Trump's language some of which was widely considered racist such as ("China virus" and "kung flu") but, according to [Vox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vox_\(website\)) reporter Terry Nguyen, many Vietnamese voters (and especially elderly, South Vietnamese migrants who populated coastal centers in the 1970s) appreciated his strong anti-China stance.[^cite_note-371]

#### Indian American voters

Data from [FiveThirtyEight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FiveThirtyEight) indicated 65% of Indian American voters backed Joe Biden, and 28% supported Donald Trump.[^cite_note-\:13-370] Some Indian Americans self-identified with Kamala Harris, but others approved of Donald Trump's support of Indian Prime Minister [Narendra Modi](444222.md).[^cite_note-372] In a speech given to 50,000 Indian-Americans during his [2019 visit to the US](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howdy_Modi), Modi praised Trump with remarks that were interpreted as an indirect endorsement of his candidacy.[^cite_note-373] Indian right-wing organizations like the [Hindu Sena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_Sena) had performed special [havans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Havan) and [pujas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puja_\(Hinduism\)) for Trump's electoral victory.[^cite_note-374]

#### American Indian and Alaska Native voters

Pre-election voter surveys by [Indian Country Today](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Country_Today) found 68% of [American Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas) and [Alaska Native](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Native) voters supporting Democratic nominee Joe Biden.[^cite_note-375] In particular, the [Navajo Reservation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navajo_Nation), which spans a large quadrant of eastern Arizona and western New Mexico, delivered up to 97% of their votes per precinct to Biden,[^cite_note-376] while overall support for Biden was between 60 and 90% on the Reservation.[^cite_note-377] Biden also posted large turnout among [Havasupai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Havasupai), [Hopi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hopi_Reservation), and [Tohono O'odham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tohono_O%CA%BCodham_Nation) peoples,[^cite_note-378] delivering a large win in New Mexico and flipping Arizona.

In Montana, while the state went for Trump overall, Biden won counties overlapping reservations of the [Blackfeet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blackfeet_Nation), [Fort Belknap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Belknap_Indian_Reservation), [Crow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crow_people) and [Northern Cheyenne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Cheyenne_Indian_Reservation).[^cite_note-\:0-379] The same pattern held in South Dakota, with most of the counties overlapping the lands of the [Standing Rock Sioux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standing_Rock_Indian_Reservation), [Cheyenne River Sioux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheyenne_River_Indian_Reservation), [Oglala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oglala) Sioux, [Rosebud Sioux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosebud_Indian_Reservation) and [Crow Creek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crow_Creek_Indian_Reservation) tribes going for Biden. For example, in [Oglala Lakota County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oglala_Lakota_County,_South_Dakota), which overlaps with the [Pine Ridge Indian Reservation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pine_Ridge_Indian_Reservation), Biden won 88% of the vote.[^cite_note-\:0-379]

Trump's strongest performance among Native tribes was with the [Lumbee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lumbee) Tribe of North Carolina, where he won a strong majority in [Robeson County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robeson_County,_North_Carolina) and flipped [Scotland County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotland_County,_North_Carolina) from Democratic to Republican.[^cite_note-\:1-380] Trump had campaigned in [Lumberton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lumberton,_North_Carolina), in Robeson County, and had promised the Lumbees [federal recognition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_federally_recognized_tribes_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-\:1-380]

### Polling accuracy

Although polls generally predicted the Biden victory, the national polls overestimated him by three to four points, and some state polling was even further from the actual result and greater than 2016's error (one or two points).[^cite_note-\:10-381] The numbers represented the highest level of error since the [1980 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_United_States_presidential_election).[^cite_note-CNN.Happened-382] This polling overestimation also applied in several Senate races, where the Democrats underperformed by about five points relative to the polls,[^cite_note-383] as well as the House elections, where Republicans gained seats instead of losing as polls predicted. Most pollsters underestimated support for Trump in several key battleground states, including Florida, Iowa, Ohio, Pennsylvania, Michigan, Texas, and Wisconsin. The discrepancy between poll predictions and the actual result persisted from the 2016 election despite pollsters' attempts to fix problems with polling in 2016, in which they underestimated the Republican vote in several states. The imprecise polls led to changes in campaigning and fundraising decisions for both Democrats and Republicans.[^cite_note-\:10-381]

According to *[The New York Times](30680.md)*, polling misses have been attributed to, among other issues, reduced average response to polling; the relative difficulty to poll certain types of voters; and pandemic-related problems, such as a theory which suggests Democrats were less willing to vote in person on Election Day than Republicans for fear of contracting COVID-19.[^cite_note-\:10-381] According to [CNN](62028.md), research presented to the [American Association for Public Opinion Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Association_for_Public_Opinion_Research) indicated one of the primary problems was an inability by pollsters to include a certain segment of Trump supporters, either due to inaccessibility or lack of participation.[^cite_note-CNN.Happened-382] *[New Statesman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Statesman)* data journalist Ben Walker pointed to Hispanics as a historically difficult group to poll accurately, leading to pollsters underestimating the level of Trump support within the demographic group.[^cite_note-384] Election analyst [Nate Silver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nate_Silver) of *[FiveThirtyEight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FiveThirtyEight)* argued that the polling error in 2020 was normal by historical standards.[^cite_note-385]

[Siena College Research Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siena_College_Research_Institute) reported that a significant source of polling error was the discounting of partial responses by "mistrustful Trump supporters" who "yelled" at their callers; when someone would "say 'I'm voting for Trump—fuck you,' and then hang up before completing the rest of the survey," it would not be counted as a response. Such "partials" made up "nearly half of Siena's error rate."[^cite_note-386]

## Aftermath

See also: [Attempts to overturn the 2020 United States presidential election](65905202.md)

### Election night



Voters cast ballots at [Roosevelt High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roosevelt_High_School_\(Iowa\)) in [Des Moines, Iowa](9163.md)

Election night, November 3, ended without a clear winner, as many state results were too close to call and millions of votes remained uncounted, including in the battleground states of Wisconsin, Michigan, Pennsylvania, North Carolina, Georgia, Arizona, and Nevada.[^cite_note-387] Results were delayed in these states due to local rules on counting mail-in ballots.[^cite_note-BrenannCenter-388] Mail-in ballots became particularly prevalent in the 2020 election due to the widespread outbreak of the COVID-19 pandemic. Over roughly 67 million mail-in ballots were submitted, over doubling the previous election's 33.5 million.[^cite_note-389] In a victory declared after midnight, Trump won the swing state of Florida by over three percentage points, an increase from his 1.2 percentage point margin in 2016, having seen significant gains in support among the Latino community in [Miami-Dade County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami-Dade_County,_Florida).[^cite_note-390]

Shortly after 12\:30 a.m. EST, Biden made a short speech in which he urged his supporters to be patient while the votes are counted, and said he believed he was "on track to win this election".[^cite_note-391][^cite_note-392] Shortly before 2\:30 a.m. EST, Trump made a speech to a roomful of supporters, falsely asserting that he had won the election and calling for a stop to all vote counting, saying that continued counting was "a fraud on the American people" and "we will be going to the U.S. Supreme Court."[^cite_note-393][^cite_note-394] The Biden campaign denounced these attempts, claiming the Trump campaign was engaging in a "naked effort to take away the democratic rights of American citizens".[^cite_note-395]

### Late counting

In Pennsylvania, where the counting of mail-in ballots began on election night, Trump prematurely declared victory on November 4 with a lead of 675,000 votes, despite more than a million ballots remaining uncounted. Trump also declared victory in North Carolina and Georgia, despite many ballots being uncounted.[^cite_note-396] At 11\:20 p.m. EST on election night, Fox News projected Biden would win Arizona, with the Associated Press making the same call at 2\:50 a.m. EST on November 4;[^cite_note-397][^cite_note-398] several other media outlets concluded the state was too close to call.[^cite_note-399][^cite_note-400] By the evening of November 4, the Associated Press reported that Biden had secured 264 electoral votes by winning Michigan and Wisconsin, with Pennsylvania, North Carolina, Georgia, and Nevada remaining uncalled.[^cite_note-apwins-401] Biden had a 1% lead in Nevada[^cite_note-402] and maintained a 2.3% lead in Arizona by November 5,[^cite_note-403] needing only to win Nevada and Arizona or to win Pennsylvania to obtain the necessary 270 electoral votes.[^cite_note-apwins-401]

Some Trump supporters expressed concerns of possible fraud after seeing the president leading in some states on Election Night, only to see Biden take the lead in subsequent days. Election experts[^cite_note-404] attributed this to several factors, including a "[red mirage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_shift_\(politics\))" of early results being counted in relatively thinly populated rural areas that favored Trump, which are quicker to count, followed later by results from more heavily populated urban areas that favored Biden, which take longer to count. In some states like Pennsylvania, Michigan and Wisconsin, Republican-controlled legislatures prohibited mail-in ballots from being counted before Election Day, and once those ballots were counted they generally favored Biden, at least in part because Trump had for months raised concerns about mail-in ballots, encouraging his supporters instead to vote in person. By contrast, in states such as Florida, which allowed counting of mail-in ballots for weeks prior to Election Day, an early [blue shift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_shift_\(politics\)) giving the appearance of a Biden lead was later overcome by in-person voting that favored Trump, resulting in the state being called for the president on Election Night.[^cite_note-NBCMirage-405][^cite_note-406][^cite_note-407]

On November 5, a federal judge dismissed a lawsuit by the Trump campaign to stop vote-counting in Pennsylvania. The Trump campaign had alleged that its observers were not given access to observe the vote, but its lawyers admitted during the hearing that its observers were already present in the vote-counting room.[^cite_note-408] Also that day, a state judge dismissed another lawsuit by the Trump campaign which alleged that in Georgia, late-arriving ballots were counted. The judge ruled no evidence had been produced that the ballots were late.[^cite_note-409] Meanwhile, a state judge in Michigan dismissed the Trump campaign's lawsuit requesting a pause in vote-counting to allow access to observers, as the judge noted that vote-counting had already finished in Michigan.[^cite_note-410] That judge also noted the official complaint did not state "why", "when, where, or by whom" an election observer was allegedly blocked from observing ballot-counting in Michigan.[^cite_note-411]

On November 6, Biden assumed leads in Pennsylvania and Georgia as the states continued to count ballots, and absentee votes in those states heavily favored Biden.[^cite_note-412] Due to the slim margin between Biden and Trump in the state, [Georgia Secretary of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_Secretary_of_State) [Brad Raffensperger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brad_Raffensperger) announced on November 6 that a recount would be held in Georgia. At that point, Georgia had not seen "any widespread irregularities" in this election, according to the voting system manager of the state, [Gabriel Sterling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabriel_Sterling).[^cite_note-413]

Also, on November 6, U.S. Supreme Court Justice [Samuel Alito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Alito) issued an order requiring officials in Pennsylvania to segregate late-arriving ballots, amid a dispute as to whether the state's Supreme Court validly ordered a 3-day extension of the deadline for mail-in ballots to arrive.[^cite_note-414] Several Republican attorneys general filed amicus briefs before the U.S. Supreme Court in subsequent days agreeing with the Pennsylvania Republican Party's view that only the state legislature could change the voting deadline.[^cite_note-415]

By November 7, several prominent Republicans had publicly denounced Trump's claims of electoral fraud, saying they were unsubstantiated, baseless or without evidence, damaging to the election process, undermining democracy and dangerous to political stability while others supported his demand of transparency.[^cite_note-416] According to [CNN](62028.md), people close to Donald Trump, such as his son-in-law and senior adviser [Jared Kushner](6191053.md) and his wife [Melania Trump](1209075.md), urged him to accept his defeat. While Donald Trump privately acknowledged the outcome of the presidential election, he nonetheless encouraged his legal team to continue pursuing legal challenges.[^cite_note-417] Trump expected to win the election in Arizona, but when Fox News declared Biden the victor of the state, Trump became furious and claimed the result was due to fraud.[^cite_note-WP20201128RuckerParker-418] Trump and his allies suffered approximately 50 legal losses in four weeks after starting their litigation.[^cite_note-419] In view of these legal defeats, Trump began to employ "a public pressure campaign on state and local Republican officials to manipulate the electoral system on his behalf".[^cite_note-WP20201128RuckerParker-418][^cite_note-420][^cite_note-421][^cite_note-422]

### Election protests

Main article: [Demonstrations in support of Donald Trump § 2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demonstrations_in_support_of_Donald_Trump#2021)



Spontaneous celebration of Trump's loss at [Frederick Douglass Circle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_Douglass_Circle) in New York City on November 7, 2020

Protests against Trump's challenges to the election results occurred in Minneapolis, Portland, New York, and other cities. Police in Minneapolis arrested more than 600 demonstrators for blocking traffic on an interstate highway. In Portland, the National Guard was called out after some protesters smashed windows and threw objects at police.[^cite_note-Bellisle-423] At the same time, groups of Trump supporters gathered outside of election centers in Phoenix, Detroit, and Philadelphia, shouting objections to counts that showed Biden leading or gaining ground.[^cite_note-Bellisle-423] In Arizona, where Biden's lead was shrinking as more results were reported, the pro-Trump protesters mostly demanded that all remaining votes be counted, while in Michigan and Pennsylvania, where Trump's lead shrank and disappeared altogether as more results were reported, they called for the count to be stopped.[^cite_note-424]

### False claims of fraud

Main article: [Big lie § 2020 stolen election claims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_lie#2020_stolen_election_claims)

See also: [Attempts to overturn the 2020 United States presidential election](65905202.md) and [Republican reactions to Donald Trump's claims of 2020 election fraud](65776878.md)



Screenshot of a tweet from Trump's Twitter account where he repeatedly and falsely claimed he had won.

[^cite_note-425]

[^cite_note-BakerHaberman-426]



To sow election doubt, Trump escalated use of "rigged election" and "election interference" statements in advance of the 2024 election compared to the previous two elections—the statements described as part of a "heads I win; tails you cheated" rhetorical strategy.

[^cite_note-NYTimes_20240524-427]

Trump and a variety of his surrogates and supporters made a series of observably false claims that the election was fraudulent. Claims that substantial fraud was committed have been repeatedly debunked.[^cite_note-428][^cite_note-429] On November 9 and 10, *[The New York Times](30680.md)* called the offices of top election officials in every state; all 45 of those who responded said there was no evidence of fraud. Some described the election as remarkably successful considering the coronavirus pandemic, the record turnout, and the unprecedented number of [mailed ballots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postal_voting).[^cite_note-NY_Times_Officials-430] On November 12, the [Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency](59407369.md) (CISA) issued a statement calling the 2020 election "the most secure in American history" and noting "\[t]here is no evidence that any voting system deleted or lost votes, changed votes, or was in any way compromised."[^cite_note-Statement-431] Five days later, Trump fired the director of CISA, whom he had appointed in 2018.[^cite_note-432]

As ballots were still being counted two days after Election Day, Trump falsely asserted that there was "tremendous corruption and fraud going on", adding: "If you count the legal votes, I easily win. If you count the illegal votes, they can try to steal the election from us."[^cite_note-433] Trump has repeatedly claimed as suspicious that mail-in ballots showed significantly more support for Biden.[^cite_note-434] This [blue shift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_shift_\(politics\)) phenomenon is believed to occur because more Democrats than Republicans tend to vote by mail, and mail ballots are counted after Election Day in many states. Leading up to the 2020 election, the effect was predicted to be even greater than usual, as Trump's attacks on mail-in voting may have deterred Republicans from casting mail ballots.[^cite_note-NBCMirage-405]

In early January 2021, Trump falsely proclaimed that he had by rights won all 50 states in the presidential election and a 535 to 3 electoral college victory. On January 2, during his phone call to [Brad Raffensperger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brad_Raffensperger), the [Georgia Secretary of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_Secretary_of_State), Trump said, "As you know, every single state. We won every state; we won every statehouse in the country... But we won every single statehouse."[^cite_note-435] Two days later, on January 4, Trump appeared at a campaign rally in [Dalton, Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dalton,_Georgia), supporting Republican senators [David Perdue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Perdue) and [Kelly Loeffler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kelly_Loeffler). During his speech at the rally, Trump again asserted that he won "every single state", and "We win every state, and they're going to have this guy \[Biden] be President?"[^cite_note-436]

Many claims of purported voter fraud were discovered to be false or misleading. In [Fulton County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fulton_County,_Georgia), [Georgia](48830.md), the number of votes affected was 342, with no breakdown of which candidates they were for.[^cite_note-437] A [viral video](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viral_video) of a Pennsylvania poll worker filling out a ballot was found to be a case of a damaged ballot being replicated to ensure proper counting, while a video claiming to show a man taking ballots illegally to a [Detroit](8687.md) counting center was found to show a photographer transporting his equipment.[^cite_note-438][^cite_note-439] Another video of a poll watcher being turned away in [Philadelphia](50585.md) was found to be real, but the poll watcher had subsequently been allowed inside after a misunderstanding had been resolved.[^cite_note-440] A viral tweet claimed 14,000 votes in [Wayne County, Michigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayne_County,_Michigan), were cast by dead people, but the list of names included was found to be incorrect.[^cite_note-441] The Trump campaign and Fox News commentator [Tucker Carlson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tucker_Carlson) also claimed a man named James Blalock had voted in Georgia despite having died in 2006, but in fact his 94-year-old widow had registered and voted as Mrs. James Blalock.[^cite_note-442] In [Erie, Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erie,_Pennsylvania), a postal worker who claimed the postmaster had instructed postal workers to backdate ballots mailed after Election Day later admitted he had fabricated the claim. Prior to this recantation, Republican senator [Lindsey Graham](363279.md) cited the claim in a letter to the Justice Department calling for an investigation, and a [GoFundMe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GoFundMe) page created for the postal worker "patriot" raised \$136,000.[^cite_note-443]

Days after Biden had been declared the winner, White House press secretary [Kayleigh McEnany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kayleigh_McEnany) asserted without evidence that the Democratic Party was welcoming fraud and illegal voting.[^cite_note-444] Republican former speaker of the House [Newt Gingrich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newt_Gingrich) stated on Fox News, "I think that it is a corrupt, stolen election."[^cite_note-445] Appearing at a [press conference outside a Philadelphia landscaping business](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Seasons_Total_Landscaping_press_conference) as Biden was being declared the winner, Trump's personal attorney [Rudy Giuliani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudy_Giuliani) asserted without evidence that hundreds of thousands of ballots were questionable.[^cite_note-446] Responding to Giuliani, a spokesperson for Pennsylvania Attorney General [Josh Shapiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josh_Shapiro) said: "Many of the claims against the commonwealth have already been dismissed, and repeating these false attacks is reckless. No active lawsuit even alleges, and no evidence presented so far has shown, widespread problems."[^cite_note-NY_Times_Officials-430]

One week after the election, Republican [Philadelphia city commissioner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_City_Commissioners) [Al Schmidt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Schmidt) said he had not seen any evidence of widespread fraud, stating, "I have seen the most fantastical things on [social media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_media_in_the_2020_United_States_presidential_election), making completely ridiculous allegations that have no basis in fact at all and see them spread." He added that his office had examined a list of dead people who purportedly voted in Philadelphia but "not a single one of them voted in Philadelphia after they died." Trump derided Schmidt, tweeting, "He refuses to look at a mountain of corruption & dishonesty. We win!"[^cite_note-447]

Attorneys who brought accusations of voting fraud or irregularities before judges could not produce valid evidence to support the allegations. In one instance, a Trump attorney sought to have ballot counting halted in Detroit on the basis of a Republican poll watcher's claim that an unidentified person had said ballots were being backdated; [Michigan Court of Appeals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michigan_Court_of_Appeals) judge [Cynthia Stephens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cynthia_Stephens) dismissed the argument as "inadmissible hearsay within hearsay".[^cite_note-448][^cite_note-449] Some senior attorneys at law firms working for Trump, notably [Jones Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jones_Day), expressed concerns that they were undermining the integrity of American elections by advancing arguments without evidence.[^cite_note-450]

Trump and his lawyers Giuliani and [Sidney Powell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sidney_Powell) repeatedly made the false claim that the [Toronto](64646.md), [Ontario](22218.md)-based firm [Dominion Voting Systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion_Voting_Systems), which had supplied voting machines for 27 states, was a "[communist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist)" organization controlled by billionaire [George Soros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Soros), former Venezuelan president [Hugo Chávez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez) (who died in 2013), or the [Chinese Communist Party](7175.md), and that the machines had "stolen" hundreds of thousands of votes from Trump. Defamatory rumors about the company circulated on social media, amplified by more than a dozen tweets or retweets by Trump. The [disinformation](142690.md) campaign prompted threats and harassment against Dominion employees.[^cite_note-451]

A December 2020 poll showed 77% of Republicans believed widespread fraud occurred during the election, along with 35% of independent voters.[^cite_note-452] Overall, 60% of Americans believed Biden's win was legitimate, 34% did not, and 6% were unsure. Another poll taken in late December showed a similar result, with 62% of Americans polled believing Biden was the legitimate winner of the election, while 37% did not.[^cite_note-453] This split in popular opinion remained largely stable, with a January 10, 2021, poll commissioned by ABC News showing 68% of Americans believed Biden's win was legitimate and 32% did not.[^cite_note-454] These numbers remained largely stagnant, with a June 2021 poll from Monmouth showing 61% believed Biden won fair and square, 32% believed he won due to fraud, and 7% were unsure.[^cite_note-455] More than a year later, public opinion on the matter still remained stagnant, with a poll commissioned by ABC News finding that 65% of Americans believed Biden's win was legitimate, 33% believed it was not legitimate, and 2% were unsure. The same poll also found that 72% of Americans thought the people involved in the attack on the U.S. Capitol on January 6, 2021, were attacking democracy, while 25% thought they were protecting democracy, and 3% were unsure.[^cite_note-456] A March 2022 poll commissioned by the conservative [Rasmussen Reports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rasmussen_Reports) found that 52% of voters think that it is likely that cheating "affected the outcome of the 2020 presidential election." while 40% of voters believe that it is unlikely. 33% say that cheating was very likely, 19% say it was somewhat likely, 13% say it was somewhat unlikely, and 27% say it was very unlikely.[^cite_note-457]

Motivated by the myth of widespread fraud, Republican state lawmakers initiated a [push to make voting laws more restrictive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_efforts_to_restrict_voting_following_the_2020_presidential_election).[^cite_note-458]

On December 13, 2024, just over a month after the [2024 United States presidential election](59568546.md), the [Public Religion Research Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Religion_Research_Institute) published the results of a survey conducted shortly after that election, between November 8 and December 2. The survey focused primarily on the 2024 election but included questions about the claim that the 2020 election was stolen from Trump: 63 percent of Republicans and 31 percent of voters overall still agreed that the 2020 election had been stolen from Trump.[^cite_note-459]

### Lawsuits

Main article: [Post-election lawsuits related to the 2020 United States presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-election_lawsuits_related_to_the_2020_United_States_presidential_election)

After the election, the [Trump campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_2020_presidential_campaign) filed lawsuits in multiple states, including Georgia, Michigan, Nevada, and Pennsylvania.[^cite_note-bloomberg-law-davis-list-460] Lawyers and other observers noted the suits were unlikely to affect the outcome. [Loyola Law School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loyola_Law_School) professor Justin Levitt said, "There's literally nothing that I've seen yet with the meaningful potential to affect the final result."[^cite_note-Time-461] Some law firms moved to drop their representation in lawsuits challenging results of the election.[^cite_note-462]

Trump unsuccessfully sought to overturn Biden's win in Georgia through litigation; suits by the Trump campaign and allies were rejected by both the [Georgia Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Georgia_\(U.S._state\))[^cite_note-463] and by federal courts.[^cite_note-Duster-464][^cite_note-465] Trump also sought to overturn Biden's win by pressuring Kemp to call a special session of the [Georgia General Assembly](426277.md) so state legislators could override the Georgia election results and appoint a pro-Trump slate of electors, an entreaty rebuffed by Kemp.[^cite_note-466]

On December 20, Giuliani filed a petition with the U.S. Supreme Court, asking them to overturn the results of the Pennsylvania election and direct the state legislature to appoint electors. The Supreme Court was regarded as very unlikely to grant this petition, and in any case Biden would still have a majority of Electoral College votes without Pennsylvania.[^cite_note-YH-20201220-467] The Court set the deadline for reply briefs from the respondents for January 22, 2021, two days after [President Elect Biden's inauguration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inauguration_of_Joe_Biden).[^cite_note-foxnews-468]

#### *Texas v. Pennsylvania*

Main article: [Texas v. Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_v._Pennsylvania)

On December 9, [Ken Paxton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ken_Paxton), the Attorney General of Texas, filed a lawsuit in the Supreme Court, asking the court to overturn the results in Michigan, Pennsylvania, Wisconsin, and Georgia. Attorneys general of seventeen other states also signed onto the lawsuit.[^cite_note-469][^cite_note-SFC-20201210-470][^cite_note-NYDN-20201210-471] In the House of Representatives, 126 Republicans—more than two-thirds of the Republican caucus—signed an [amicus brief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amicus_brief) in support of the lawsuit.[^cite_note-472] The suit was rejected by the Supreme Court on December 11, due to a lack of [standing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standing_\(law\)).[^cite_note-473][^cite_note-474]

### Trump's refusal to concede

Main article: [Attempts to overturn the 2020 United States presidential election § Refusal to accept 2020 electoral loss](65905202.md#Refusal_to_accept_2020_electoral_loss)



&#x20;CNN fact checker [Daniel Dale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Dale) reported that through June 9, 2021, Trump had issued 132 written statements since leaving office, of which "a third have included lies about the election"—more than any other subject.

[^cite_note-CNN_20210612-475]

Early in the morning on November 4, with vote counts still going on in many states, Trump claimed he had won: "This is an embarrassment to our country. We were getting ready to win this election, frankly we did win this election."[^cite_note-476] For weeks after the networks had called the election for Biden, Trump refused to [acknowledge that Biden had won](2538448.md). Unlike every [losing major party presidential candidate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_unsuccessful_major_party_candidates_for_President_of_the_United_States) before him, Trump refused to formally concede, breaking with the tradition of formal concession started in 1896, when [William Jennings Bryan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Jennings_Bryan) sent a congratulatory telegram to President-elect [William McKinley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_McKinley).[^cite_note-NatGeo_2020-477] Biden described Trump's refusal as "an embarrassment".[^cite_note-478]

In the wake of the election, Trump's White House ordered government agencies not to cooperate with the Biden transition team in any way,[^cite_note-479] and the [General Services Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Services_Administration) (GSA) refused to formally acknowledge Biden's victory.[^cite_note-480]

Trump finally acknowledged Biden's victory in a tweet on November 15, although he refused to concede and blamed his loss on fraud, stating: "He won because the Election was Rigged." Trump then tweeted: "I concede NOTHING! We have a long way to go."[^cite_note-481][^cite_note-482]

In a June 2021 interview with [Sean Hannity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sean_Hannity), Trump stated that "we didn't win" and said that he wished President Biden success in international diplomacy, which *[Forbes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forbes)* declared as Trump "\[coming] as close as he's ever been to conceding his 2020 election loss."[^cite_note-483]

### GSA delays certifying Biden as president-elect

Main article: [Presidential transition of Joe Biden § Delays in initiating the transition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_transition_of_Joe_Biden#Delays_in_initiating_the_transition)

Although all major media outlets called the election for Biden on November 7, the head of the [General Services Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Services_Administration) (GSA), Trump appointee [Emily W. Murphy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emily_W._Murphy), refused for over two weeks to certify Biden as the president-elect. Without formal GSA ascertainment of the winner, the official transition process was delayed.[^cite_note-GSA-484] On November 23, Murphy acknowledged Biden as the winner and said the Trump administration would begin the transition. Trump said he had instructed his administration to "do what needs to be done" but did not concede, and indicated he would continue his fight to overturn the election results.[^cite_note-Holmes-485]

### Attempts to delay or deny election results

Main article: [Attempts to overturn the 2020 United States presidential election](65905202.md)



*[Texas v. Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_v._Pennsylvania)* motion (left), which called for the Supreme Court to nullify the election, and *[amicus curiae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amicus_curiae)* brief from 17 states (right)

In November, Trump focused his efforts on trying to delay vote certifications at the county and state level.[^cite_note-486] On December 2, Trump posted a 46-minute video to his [social media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_media_in_the_2020_United_States_presidential_election) in which he repeated his baseless claims that the election was "rigged" and fraudulent, and he called for either state legislatures or courts to overturn the election and allow him to stay in office.[^cite_note-487] He continued to pressure elected Republicans in Michigan, Georgia, and Pennsylvania in an unprecedented attempt to overturn his loss. Some commentators have characterized Trump's actions as an attempted [coup d'état](25914154.md) or [self-coup](1221872.md).[^cite_note-coup-bundle-488]

On December 15, the day after the electoral college vote, Republican Senate Majority leader [Mitch McConnell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitch_McConnell), who was previously said he would not recognize the election results, publicly accepted Biden's win, saying, "Today, I want to congratulate President-elect Joe Biden."[^cite_note-489]

A December 18 meeting in the White House discussed [Michael Flynn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Flynn)'s suggestion to overturn the election by invoking [martial law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martial_law_in_the_United_States) and rerunning the election in several swing states under military supervision.[^cite_note-CNN-20201219-490][^cite_note-NYT-20201219-491][^cite_note-POL-20201219-492] Army Secretary [Ryan D. McCarthy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ryan_D._McCarthy) and Army Chief of Staff General [James McConville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_McConville) later issued a joint statement saying: "There is no role for the U.S. military in determining the outcome of an American election."[^cite_note-WT-20201218-493] In a December 20 tweet, Trump dismissed accusations that he wanted to declare martial law as "fake news".[^cite_note-CNN-20201220-494]

In a December 21 news conference, outgoing attorney general [William Barr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Barr) disavowed several actions reportedly being considered by Trump, including seizing voting machines, appointing a special counsel to investigate voter fraud, and appointing one to investigate [Hunter Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter_Biden).[^cite_note-WP-20201221mz-495]

### Plot for state legislatures to choose electors

Main article: [Trump fake electors plot § Plot for state legislatures to choose electors](71378172.md#Plot_for_state_legislatures_to_choose_electors)

Both before and after the election, Trump and other Republican leaders publicly considered asking certain Republican-controlled state legislatures to select [presidential electors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Electoral_College) favoring Trump, even if Biden won the popular vote in those states.[^cite_note-NYTTrumpElector-496] In Pennsylvania, a state which Biden won, the president's personal lawyer Rudy Giuliani asked a federal judge to consider allowing the Republican-controlled state legislature to select electors.[^cite_note-497] Legal experts, including New York University law professor Richard Pildes, have raised numerous legal and political objections to this policy, noting that in various battleground states, Democratic Party members holding statewide office would thwart such efforts,[^cite_note-GuardianTrumpElector-498] and ultimately Congress would probably reject the votes of legislatively appointed electors over those elected by the voters.[^cite_note-499] Law professor [Lawrence Lessig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawrence_Lessig) noted that while the Constitution grants state legislatures the power to determine how electors are selected, including the power to directly appoint them, [Article II, Section 1, Clause 4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_II,_Section_1,_Clause_4) gives Congress the power to determine when electors must be appointed, which they have designated to be [Election Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_Day_\(United_States\)), meaning that legislatures cannot change how electors are appointed for an election after this date.[^cite_note-500] In modern times, most states have used a popular vote within their state as the determining factor in who gets all the state's electors,[^cite_note-GuardianTrumpElector-498] and changing election rules after an election could also violate the Constitution's [Due Process Clause](629693.md).[^cite_note-VoxTrumpElector-501]

### Pressure on state and local officials

Main article: [Attempts to overturn the 2020 United States presidential election § Trump's attempt to pressure state officials](65905202.md#Trump's_attempt_to_pressure_state_officials)

As the Trump campaign's lawsuits were repeatedly rejected in court, Trump personally communicated with Republican local and state officials in at least three states, including state legislators, attorneys general, and governors who had supported him during and after the elections. He pressured them to overturn the election results in their states by recounting votes, throwing out certain votes, or getting the state legislature to replace the elected Democratic slate of Electoral College members with a Republican slate of electors chosen by the legislature.[^cite_note-Gardner-502] In late November, he personally phoned Republican members of two county electoral boards in Michigan, urging them to reverse their vote certifications.[^cite_note-503] He invited members of the Michigan state legislature to the White House, where they declined his suggestion that they choose a new slate of electors.[^cite_note-504] He repeatedly spoke to the Republican governor of Georgia and the secretary of state, demanding that they reverse their state's election results, and retaliating when they did not, strongly criticizing them in speeches and tweets, and demanding that the governor resign.[^cite_note-505]

During the first week of December, Trump twice phoned the [speaker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speaker_of_the_Pennsylvania_House_of_Representatives) of the [Pennsylvania state House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_House_of_Representatives), urging him to appoint a replacement slate of electors; the speaker said he did not have that power but later joined in a letter encouraging the state's representatives in Congress to dispute the results.[^cite_note-Gardner-502] On January 4 *The Washington Post* reported that in a phone call on January 2, Trump pressured Georgia secretary of state [Brad Raffensperger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brad_Raffensperger) to overturn the state's result, telling him "I just want to find 11,780 votes" and threatening him with legal action if he did not cooperate.[^cite_note-WaPo_Georgia_call-506][^cite_note-NYT_Georgia_call-507] On January 4, 2021, Democratic congressional leaders, believing Trump "engaged in solicitation of, or conspiracy to commit, a number of election crimes," requested the FBI to investigate the incident.[^cite_note-NBC-20210104-508] In addition, while some House Republicans tried to defend Trump's Georgia call, Democrats began drafting a [censure resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Censure_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-CNN-20210104-509] Two months later *The Washington Post* acknowledged that they had misquoted Trump, and added a correction to the article.[^cite_note-510] Also on January 2, 2021, Trump took part in a mass phone call with nearly 300 state legislators from Michigan, Pennsylvania, and Wisconsin, in which he urged them to "decertify" the election results in their states.[^cite_note-511]

### Election recounts

Main articles: [2020 United States presidential election in Arizona § Audits and Recounts by Election Officials](59561924.md#Audits_and_Recounts_by_Election_Officials), [2020 United States presidential election in Georgia § Statewide audit and recount](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election_in_Georgia#Statewide_audit_and_recount), and [2020 United States presidential election in Wisconsin § Aftermath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election_in_Wisconsin#Aftermath)

On November 11, Georgia Secretary of State [Brad Raffensperger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brad_Raffensperger) ordered a statewide hand recount of the vote in addition to the normal audit process. At the time, Biden held a lead of 13,558 votes.[^cite_note-512] The hand recount was concluded on November 19 and affirmed Biden's lead by 12,284 votes. Therefore, the hand recount netted Trump 1,274 votes. The change in the count was due to a number of human errors, including memory cards that did not upload properly to the state servers, and was not attributable to any fraud in the original tally.[^cite_note-NY_Times_interactive-513][^cite_note-514] The Trump campaign requested a machine recount, which was estimated to cost taxpayers \$200,000 in one Georgia county alone.[^cite_note-515] On December 7, Biden was confirmed as the winner of the machine recount requested by Trump's campaign.[^cite_note-Duster-464]

On November 18, the Trump campaign wired \$3 million to pay for partial recounts in [Milwaukee County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee_County,_Wisconsin) and [Dane County, Wisconsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dane_County,_Wisconsin), where Milwaukee and Madison, the two largest cities in the state and Democratic strongholds, are located.[^cite_note-516][^cite_note-517] During the recount, Milwaukee County election commissioner Tim Posnanski said several Republican observers were breaking rules by posing as independents.[^cite_note-518] The recount started November 20 and concluded on November 29, increasing Biden's lead by 87 votes.[^cite_note-519]

### Electoral College votes

The [presidential electors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Electoral_College) met in the state capitol of each state and in the District of Columbia on December 14, 2020, and formalized Biden's victory, casting 306 votes for Biden/Harris and 232 votes for Trump/Pence.[^cite_note-APOfficial-520][^cite_note-WillisDuda-521] Unlike the 2016 election, there were no [faithless electors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faithless_elector).[^cite_note-522] In six [swing states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swing_state) won by Biden, groups of self-appointed Republican "alternate electors" met on the same day to vote for Trump. These alternate slates were not signed by the governors of the states they claim to represent, did not have the backing of any state legislature, and have no legal status.[^cite_note-WillisDuda-521][^cite_note-523]

Even after the casting of the electoral votes and rejection of his lawsuits seeking to overturn the election by at least 86 judges,[^cite_note-WillisDuda-521] Trump refused to concede defeat.[^cite_note-APOfficial-520][^cite_note-WillisDuda-521][^cite_note-Easley-524] In a speech following the Electoral College vote, Biden praised the resiliency of U.S. democratic institutions and the high election turnout (calling it "one of the most amazing demonstrations of civic duty we've ever seen in our country") and called for national unity. Biden also condemned Trump, and those who backed his efforts to subvert the election outcome, for adopting a stance "so extreme that we've never seen it before – a position that refused to respect the will of the people, refused to respect the rule of law and refused to honor our Constitution" and for exposing state election workers and officials to "political pressure, verbal abuse and even threats of physical violence" that was "simply unconscionable".[^cite_note-525][^cite_note-Easley-524]

The [117th United States Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/117th_United_States_Congress) first convened on January 3, 2021, and was scheduled to count and certify the Electoral College votes on January 6, 2021. There were 222 Democrats and 212 Republicans in the House; there were 51 Republicans, 46 Democrats, and two independents in the Senate. Several Republican members of the House and Senate said they would raise objections to the reported count in several states,[^cite_note-526][^cite_note-WP-20210105-527] meeting the requirement that if a member from each body objects, the two houses must meet separately to discuss whether to accept the certified state vote.[^cite_note-528][^cite_note-529] A statement from the vice president's office said Pence welcomes the plan by Republicans to "raise objections and bring forward evidence" challenging the election results.[^cite_note-NYT-20210102lb-530]

On December 28, 2020, Representative [Louie Gohmert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louie_Gohmert) filed a lawsuit in Texas challenging the [constitutionality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutionality) of the [Electoral Count Act of 1887](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_Count_Act_of_1887), claiming Vice President Pence has the power and ability to unilaterally decide which slates of electoral votes get counted.[^cite_note-531][^cite_note-532] The case was dismissed on January 1, 2021, for lack of both standing and jurisdiction.[^cite_note-gohmert_dismissal-533][^cite_note-NYT-20210101-534] The plaintiffs filed an appeal, and the appeal was dismissed by a three-judge panel of the appeals court the next day.[^cite_note-TH-20210102-535]

As vice president, Pence was due to preside over the January 6, 2021, congressional session to count the electoral votes, which is normally a non-controversial, ceremonial event. In January 2021, Trump began to pressure Pence to take action to overturn the election, demanding both in public and in private that Pence use his position to overturn the election results in swing states and declare Trump and Pence the winners of the election.[^cite_note-536] Pence demurred that the law does not give him that power.[^cite_note-537]

Starting in December, Trump called for his supporters to stage a massive protest in Washington, D.C., on January 6 to argue against certification of the electoral vote, using tweets such as "Big protest in D.C. on January 6th. Be there, will be wild!"[^cite_note-WP-20201230-538] D.C. police were concerned, and the National Guard was alerted because several rallies in December had turned violent.[^cite_note-WP-20201230-538]

On January 6, 2021, shortly after Trump continued to press false claims of election fraud at a [rally](66307490.md#January_6_Trump_rally) on the [Ellipse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Ellipse) in Washington, D.C., a crowd of Trump supporters [stormed the United States Capitol](66307490.md), interrupting the [Joint session of the United States Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_session_of_the_United_States_Congress) where the Electoral College ballots were being certified and forcing lawmakers to flee the chamber. As part of an organized effort by Republican lawmakers to challenge the results in close states, the House and the Senate were meeting separately to debate the results of Arizona's election and accepting the electoral college ballots submitted. Several other challenges were also planned. Congress reconvened that same night, after the Capitol was cleared of trespassers, and leaders of both parties, including Vice President Mike Pence, House Speaker [Nancy Pelosi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nancy_Pelosi), and Senate Speaker Mitch McConnell urged the legislators to confirm the electors. The Senate resumed its session at around 8\:00 p.m. to finish debating the objection to the Arizona and Pennsylvania electors were also considered. The joint session completed its work shortly before 4\:00 a.m. on Thursday, January 7, declaring Biden and Harris the winners.[^cite_note-539][^cite_note-540][^cite_note-541][^cite_note-542][^cite_note-543][^cite_note-544][^cite_note-545]

The rioters entered the House and Senate chambers and vandalized offices. Five people died as a result: one person was shot by police, one [Capitol Police](1915214.md) officer died from a stroke after fisticuffs with rioters,[^cite_note-546] one person died of a heart attack, another of a stroke, and the final death is still under investigation. Trump was accused of inciting the violence with his rhetoric,[^cite_note-547] an accusation reinforced with an [article of impeachment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_impeachment_of_Donald_Trump) on January 13 for "incitement of insurrection".[^cite_note-548] Several commentators viewed the attack on the Capitol Building as an indicator of political instability that could lead to political violence in future elections, ranging from [domestic terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_terrorism) to a second American Civil War.[^cite_note-549][^cite_note-550][^cite_note-551][^cite_note-552][^cite_note-553][^cite_note-554]

### Post-certification

On May 10, 2021, over 120 retired U.S. generals and admirals published an open letter alleging that there had been "election irregularities", suggesting that the election had not been "fair and honest" and did not "accurately reflect the "will of the people", and arguing for tighter restrictions on voting.[^cite_note-555] On May 12, 2021, U.S. Representative [Liz Cheney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liz_Cheney) was removed from her party leadership role as Chair of the [House Republican Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_Republican_Conference), partially for continuing to assert that the election had been fair and that the election results were final.[^cite_note-556][^cite_note-\:22-557][^cite_note-558]

Well into Biden's presidency, Trump continued to insist that he had actually won the 2020 election.[^cite_note-559] As of August 2021, surveys found that a majority of Republicans believe Trump had won the 2020 election.[^cite_note-560] A widespread rumor predicted that Trump would be somehow reinstated to the presidency in August 2021, although the predicted date of August 13 passed without incident.[^cite_note-561]

### Election audits

Further information: [Attempts to overturn the 2020 United States presidential election § Election audits](65905202.md#Election_audits)

On March 31, 2021, the Republican caucus of the [Arizona State Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arizona_State_Senate) hired several outside firms to [examine the results](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Maricopa_County_presidential_ballot_audit) of the presidential and senatorial elections in [Maricopa County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maricopa_County,_Arizona), where Biden had won by a large margin.[^cite_note-AP_May_10-562] There had been three previous audits and recounts of that county's results.[^cite_note-563] The examination was initially funded by \$150,000 from the State Senate operating budget; additional funding was to come from outside sources.[^cite_note-564] In July the lead firm conducting the review released a summary of major donors, indicating \$5.7 million was raised from five groups associated with individuals who had [cast doubt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_denial_movement_in_the_United_States) on the presidential election.[^cite_note-565] The audit began on April 22, 2021, and was expected to last 60 days.[^cite_note-566] The investigation was still ongoing in August when a judge issued an order for the release of documents.[^cite_note-567] On September 24, a preliminary release of the audit claimed to have found minor discrepancies in the original, state-certified count, which had actually widened Biden's margin by 360 votes.[^cite_note-568]

[^cite_note-ElectionDay-1]: &#x20;About 64% of voters [voted early](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_voting) before November 3 in person or [by mail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postal_voting_in_the_United_States), with the earliest state starting on September 4.<sup>[\[7\]](#cite_note-10)</sup><sup>[\[8\]](#cite_note-EarlyVote2020-11)</sup>

[^cite_note-7]: &#x20;The Federal Election Commission calculated a voter turnout of 62.8% in 2020, as the votes for president divided by the estimated U.S. population at or over age 18.<sup>[\[1\]](#cite_note-FEC-2)</sup> The denominator included U.S. residents ineligible to vote due to not being U.S. citizens or due to a criminal conviction, and excluded U.S. citizens residing in other countries who were eligible to vote. This turnout was an increase of 7.1pp compared to the turnout of 55.7% in the 2016 election, calculated by the same institution with the same basis.<sup>[\[2\]](#cite_note-3)</sup>\
    \
    &#x20;The U.S. Census Bureau calculated a voter turnout of 66.8% in 2020, as the people reporting having voted divided by the estimated U.S. population at or over age 18 who were U.S. citizens. The denominator excluded U.S. residents ineligible to vote due to not being U.S. citizens, but included those ineligible due to a criminal conviction and excluded U.S. citizens residing in other countries who were eligible to vote. This turnout was an increase of 5.4pp compared to the turnout of 61.4% in the 2016 election, calculated by the same institution with the same basis.<sup>[\[3\]](#cite_note-4)</sup>\
    \
    &#x20;The U.S. Elections Project calculated a voter turnout of 66.6% in 2020, as the total ballots divided by the estimated population that was eligible to vote.<sup>[\[4\]](#cite_note-Elections_Project_2020g-5)</sup> The denominator excluded U.S. residents ineligible to vote due to not being U.S. citizens or due to a criminal conviction, and included U.S. citizens residing in other countries who were eligible to vote. This turnout was an increase of 6.5pp compared to the turnout of 60.1% in the 2016 election, calculated by the same institution with the same basis.<sup>[\[5\]](#cite_note-6)</sup>

[^cite_note-Florida_Residency-9]: &#x20;Trump's official state of residence was [New York](8210131.md) in the [2016 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election), but it was changed to [Florida](18933066.md) when his permanent residence was switched from [Trump Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trump_Tower) to [Mar-a-Lago](1452754.md) in 2019.<sup>[\[6\]](#cite_note-8)</sup>

[^cite_note-15]: &#x20;The previous two female vice presidential nominees were [Geraldine Ferraro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geraldine_Ferraro) in [1984](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_United_States_presidential_election) and [Sarah Palin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarah_Palin) in [2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_United_States_presidential_election).

[^cite_note-54]: &#x20;Although claimed in Hawkins's campaign website, he did not obtain write-in access in Montana.<sup>[\[49\]](#cite_note-53)</sup>

[^cite_note-55]: &#x20;Candidates in **bold** were listed on ballots of states representing most of the [electoral college](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Electoral_College). Other candidates were listed on ballots of more than one state **and** were listed on ballots or were [write-in candidates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Write-in_candidate) in states representing most of the electoral college.

[^cite_note-56]: &#x20;In some states, some presidential candidates were listed with a different or no vice presidential candidate.

[^cite_note-57]: &#x20;In some states, some candidates were listed with a different or additional party, a label, or as [independent](2172378.md) or unaffiliated.

[^cite_note-77]: &#x20;[Andrew Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Johnson) received votes during the [1868 Democratic National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1868_Democratic_National_Convention), four months after having been [impeached](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impeachment_of_Andrew_Johnson).<sup>[\[68\]](#cite_note-76)</sup>

[^cite_note-second_debate-241]: &#x20;Following the cancellation of the planned second debate on October 9, both candidates held separate but simultaneous televised [town hall events](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Town_hall_meeting) on the intended date of October 15. Trump's was broadcast on NBC, moderated by [Savannah Guthrie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savannah_Guthrie), while Biden's was on ABC, moderated by [George Stephanopoulos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Stephanopoulos).<sup>[\[231\]](#cite_note-240)</sup>

[^cite_note-calc-245]: &#x20;Calculated by taking the difference of 100% and all other candidates combined.

[^cite_note-264]: &#x20;Tossup: 50%–59%, Lean: 60%–74%, Likely: 75%–94%, Solid: 95%–100%

[^cite_note-304]: &#x20;Although [Ronald Reagan](25433.md) in [1980](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_United_States_presidential_election) and [Bill Clinton](3356.md) in [1992](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_United_States_presidential_election) defeated their incumbent opponents by wider popular-vote margins than Biden's, their shares of the vote were kept lower by substantial [third-party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_party_\(United_States\)) voting.

[^cite_note-310]: &#x20;Republicans [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush) in [1988](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_United_States_presidential_election) and [George W. Bush](3414021.md) in [2000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_United_States_presidential_election) won the presidency without [Iowa](26810748.md).

Hawkins/Walker may have received write-in votes, which were not reported individually and are also included in *others*.

[^cite_note-297]: &#x20;In every presidential election from [1788–89](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1788%E2%80%9389_United_States_presidential_election) through [1828](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1828_United_States_presidential_election), multiple state legislatures selected their presidential electors by discretionary appointment rather than upon the results of a poll, while the [South Carolina General Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Carolina_General_Assembly) did so in [every presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_presidential_elections_in_South_Carolina) through [1860](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1860_United_States_presidential_election) and the [Colorado General Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_General_Assembly) selected its [state's electors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1876_United_States_presidential_election_in_Colorado) by discretionary appointment in the [1876 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1876_United_States_presidential_election).
